he performs tasks just as he wishes.

The supreme letter called ha,
without its head and adorned with a bindu:
all of its rites without exception,
beneficial ones, I shall explain. Listen!
It is empty of pervaded and pervader,
all-going and indestructible,
utterly empty and empty.
It is mindless, asleep,
and free from waking;
nothing at all appears to it.
It is not gods, asuras or yakṣas,
not kinnaras or gandharvas,
not nāgas or rākṣasas,
not the ocean or Mount Meru,
not the trees by rivers, nor forests,
nor likewise the earth and the rest;
it is not the frightful hells and so on,
not fire, smoke and the like,
not sun, moon, planets or stars,
not humans, piśācas and the rest.
It is not the womb-born, the heat-born,
the egg-born or the moisture-born;
not what dwells on dry land or in water;
not the lotus-born,
nor Indra, Viṣṇu or Śaṅkara (Śiva);
not wind, bile, phlegm and the rest,
nor the host of elements and so on.
If one constantly contemplates
the supreme letter called ha, it does not become an object of apprehension.
If one meditates on it for a year,
old age and all diseases are destroyed.
If even poison does not spread in him,
how could there be any fear of disease?
Even if he eats a hundred loads of poison,
he will not die.
If one constantly meditates on emptiness,
one will be liberated, without doubt.

The letter kṣa without its head:
having dissolved it into the supreme reality
and meditated, clairvoyance arises in an instant.
Through the application joined with the bindu,
imagine lines entering
the passage of the palate and the nostrils.
The lines are like the tip of a lamp flame;
imagine them radiant with the four maṇḍalas.
Constantly piercing the palate, one remains;
by constantly abiding in the yoga,
in seven days one will see it.
There is nothing equal to this.
Even a brahmin-killer, in seven days,
will see the tip expand.
The yogin who sees in this way
goes at will to the towns of others and so on.
The rites of this very king kṣa
are inconceivable.
Worldly and transworldly accomplishments,
O lady of vows, I have explained to you.
Even the rites of each single one
cannot be fully taught.
It is like a wish-fulfilling jewel
that accomplishes people's own aims.
The letters of each class of letters
are each distinct,
and each has four applications,
just as one is applied in four ways.
The great seal of the vowels and consonants
bestows every accomplishment;
yet without wisdom and skillful means
the supreme state is not attained.

Thus spoke Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
in union with Mahāmāyā,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the forty-first chapter, the section on the ritual for the arising of the sādhana of the mantras of the vowels and consonants.

Then the goddesses made offerings and bowed to the Blessed One, and again they requested:

"The secret you explained before,
which abides always as the nature of all,
the great knowledge-mantra of sixteen letters,
as taught in the Saṃvara Uttara,
and the elements and likewise the channels,
their source, the way of sound and their emergence,
hard to find in the three worlds,
their divisions of beginning, middle and end,
and how they rightly abide within:
likewise, please explain their divisions."

He declared:
"Listen, goddess Mahāmāyā,
play of every illusion!
This is secret even from all the buddhas;
what need is there to mention others?
The sixteen great illusions
are explained as the mātṛkās.
Through this they have become
the inner maṇḍala, without doubt.
To the yogin's preparation of nectar
nothing comes close, not even a sixteenth part.
Further, the nectar substances
I shall now explain. Listen!
ra is the element of the great red one;
ha is what is born from the mountain;
se is the essential leaf, patra;
pa is taught to be skin;
ra is said to be fragrance;
me is the flower;
likewise ra is water;
me is pisita;
sa is kunduru resin;
re is called sari;
ātma is nalaja;
ni is scented ghee;
sa is teeth and nails;
dā is what arises from the ocean;
sthi is the essence of snow;
ta is camphor,
arising from the mind of awakening,
ever the sacred, supreme wisdom.
All the channels are perfectly gathered;
these, goddess, I have shown to you.
'Secret' and the rest, the yogin's substances,
refer to the five: feces, urine and the rest.
This is the group that gathers the elements;
thus the elixir of rejuvenation is taught.

"The passionate being, the reality of all ḍākas,
arisen from the vajra,
through the union that opens the lotus
is called Padmanarteśvara.
Because it radiates clearly upward,
it is called Vajrasūrya;
because it is clear and blazing,
it is also the Sage Mahāvairocana.
Wind arises from the manifold;
through its special activity it is Paramāśva.
Consciousness is Vajrasattva,
by nature mutually nondual.
In that place where the sun has set,
the lord of the form of the emanation body,
having gathered the bliss of gods and humans,
is unsullied,
the one who cuts the path of saṃsāra,
in whom all elaboration is stilled,
Vajrasattva Heruka:
this is explained by the words 'all ḍākinīs.'"

Alternatively, the words "all ḍākinīs" refer to Pracaṇḍā and the others, and "reality" means intrinsic nature; it is formed from their subtlest particles. Because that very one is endowed with the power to benefit sentient beings, it is expressed by the word "being." Because it destroys the mountain of ignorance, wisdom itself is the vajra.

Two kinds of sattva are taught:
the container world and the contained world.
The contained are said to be the moving,
and likewise the container is the stable.
Each of these is again twofold:
the fortunate and the unfortunate.
The forms of earth, water, fire, and wind
abide in four kinds.

"This" refers to that very Blessed One (the Buddha). Since he is self-arisen, he is the self-arisen Blessed One. He is "self-arisen" because his nature is inferred from the great elements. He is the "Blessed One" because he possesses the qualities of lordship and the rest. Or else he is the Blessed One because he has vanquished the māras, the afflictions and the rest. That self-arisen Blessed One is the one supreme god. Because he is a god and is supreme, he is the supreme god. The words "supreme god" mean what is chief and highest among the elements taught earlier: the semen and blood that bring forth the aggregates, the elements, and the sense sources.

Because supreme bliss is born and arises from it, it is the source. "Definite emergence" is the stated tradition of generating the mind. "Sound" is melody: following the melody of wisdom. "Mode" is what is designated by the sound; it arises from that. Because it is relied upon and approached, it is the samaya: the objects, such as form, that the subject establishes. Since ultimately they are indivisible, the subject, the sense faculty, is by figurative designation also called "object." "Conduct" is the moving about of those twelve faculties. The attainment of the awakening mind, which is the nature of their consciousness, is the domain of conduct.

"Hard to find in the three worlds" names the characteristic of not being found among the three contaminated things. It rightly abides in beginning, middle, and end. Of these, the beginning is the secret: "thus," the awareness that earlier became the source. The "end" is delight: what is called "heard" is to be relied upon. The "middle" is the supreme: "by me" means having become supremely excellent. "Rightly abiding" is the self of all: it is "at one time." That very Blessed Lady, the great Mahāmāyā, engages in base conduct, together with the blood that comes from the union of vajra and lotus with an eight-year-old.

"The bliss of gods and humans, compounded, of vajra particles, does not equal even a sixteenth part." Here the sixteenth part is the elements arising from a woman's thread, rolled together with sixteen threads; it is what is called great passion. Thus there are sixteen portions rolled with sixteen threads. "Compounded" means made into a pill of the essence of the five nectars; it becomes a preparation of the five nectars. "And" has the sense of gathering: with the moisture of whatever elements are gathered and taught by category, one should roll it with the seed and examine it. "The bliss of the gods" is the instant in which, that maturing like nectar, one's own body becomes vajra. "Of humans" because it belongs to humans: the bliss that is definitely manifest. It is marked by the clarity of constant bliss, the nature of all accomplishments. Actualizing the mind vajra that is evenly joined with the dharmadhātu, one is born as bliss itself through relying on bliss, because it bestows lasting bliss. It is like this example:

A seed steeped in sweetness,
when it relies on sweetness itself,
since it is brought forth by sweetness,
its taste becomes sweet.

Likewise, if one relies on the bliss arising from the nectar of the Dharma, bliss increases. Nothing equal to it exists anywhere in the three things. Let alone anything equal to it in all its portions, there is not even anything equal to a fraction of its subtle particle. The subtle particle is the nature of the subtle vajra: the unfolding of the awakening mind whose nature is bliss.

Established through power,
through essential nature,
and through intrinsic nature:
the account of its nature
I explain to you, Fair-Faced One.
Whoever delights in external union
does not equal even a sixteenth part.
Once one has seen this supreme union,
the others are like grass eaten by worms.

Now I shall explain the excellent places endowed with the qualities of the great lotus.

In the manner of obscure signs,
the places are explained as "secret."
Things that are obscure
are explained in the language of signs.
Relying here on conceptual thought,
I shall explain the secret places.

"Great wilderness" and the rest are as the words say. Further, "great wilderness" is wisdom, because it possesses immeasurable groves of trees that are the qualities of mantra. Because it is by nature the self of the deity, it is "the regions." The plural is because its nature is the infinite families. Because it is free of conceptions of self, sentient being, and the like, it is "secluded." Because those of wrong view cannot destroy it, it is a "mountain." Because it is hard to enter, it is a "ravine." Because it has become the home of the river of profound and vast wisdom, it is "the shores of the great ocean." Because the pure nature of the eightfold noble path and of generosity and the rest is established, it is "primordially established." Because the awakening of the śrāvakas and the rest is burned up by the fire of desire, it is the "charnel ground." Thus, because the five, the mirror-like and the rest, are pure, they are proclaimed as five kinds of places.

The lesser: first, sporting with the lotus of an eleven-year-old, one should accomplish the elixir. Second, sporting with a twelve-year-old, one should seek the samaya. Third, delighting together with a sixteen-year-old, one should become familiar with the mantras. Fourth, with a twenty-year-old, one should accomplish flying in the sky. Fifth, with a twenty-five-year-old, one should confer empowerment. So it goes up to that point. Thus the words "the supreme and the middling sources of relief" are to be applied in order to those five places.

There, those of good family should worship the daughters of the vajra family and the others with scented water. "With" means that the wise one, taking that girl's flower, worships together with his own semen. "Always" means everywhere, without interruption. "A particular time" means in the morning. Further, "co-emergent accomplishment" means one who possesses the common accomplishments and is loving toward the practitioner. With that drop one should worship the buddhas and bodhisattvas.

Furthermore, "the great wilderness" means the female mudrā, because she has a forest of afflictions such as desire. When that very one is thoroughly purified by skillful means, she is the path to awakening; "thoroughly purified" means brought to its final culmination. Her dharmodaya is "the place." Because that place belongs to a maiden of the vajra family and so forth, praised by all the tathāgatas, it is "secluded." Because it is unmoved by great bliss, it is "the mountain." Because it is hard for inferior beings to enter, it is "the mountain ravine," and because it has a cave, it is "the thicket." Because it is the abode of the ocean of the union of skillful means and wisdom, it is "the ocean shore." Because it abides equally in the continuum of all sentient beings, it is "the charnel ground."

"Primordially accomplished" means always accomplished. Free of rising and setting, it is perfected like space; since it is always perceived in the mode of great bliss, it has been made into that nature. The word "ni" means a gathering, because it possesses that.

In "covered with a variegated lotus cloth," "variegated" means of many kinds, and "lotus" is a code word for form. "Cloth" means not made clear, and "covered" means made completely unmanifest. "Seat" means making awareness itself the seat, and "pure" means soft. The lotuses of the goddesses are adorned with the garments and ornaments of their own signs. "Place" means one should worship: one should please again and again with desire. "Saṃvara" is supreme bliss.

"And lotus blood": likewise, blood is the elixir. The blood is the lotus, and excrement and the like are the elixir. Thereby the following is taught:

"Great blood together with camphor,
mixed with red sandalwood,
when it has entered the assembly,
is the unsurpassed rasāyana."

Here "great blood" is the menses that flow at their due time. "Red sandalwood" is flesh, and "together with" means with that. "Entered the assembly" means arisen from all the senses. The essence of all of these is the rasāyana.

As for "it should be eaten," if one asks by what rite it should be eaten: in "the thick one or the hindmost," "the thick one" is the vajra, and "the hindmost" is the bhaga, because it is designated by the name anāmā, "the nameless." "In the mouth" means with that very mouth, into the mouth of one's own deity. Moreover:

"First make an offering of it to the guru,
then offer it to the buddhas;
having given it to the mudrā,
taste it as if it were a peaceful drink."

As for "the four offerings": the meditator in the stage of generating the awakening mind should, at the four times, worship the goddesses such as the ḍākinīs, who embody the four doors of liberation, having ascertained them from the pride of emptiness up to the completed form.

"Entering into union with the hero" concerns the meditator abiding in the other stage, the completion stage. If one asks what those who abide constantly, with no fixed times, in the pride that has neither beginning nor end, should do: "with the mind absorbed within," endowed with union, just as it is, with wisdom, the mother who gives birth to all the tathāgatas, they emanate supreme joy; and so on up to "one should satisfy by abiding."

Worshiping in this way, there is the lowest, the supreme, and the intermediate. The lowest is the mode of great passion. This is to be connected with the words: "The Vajra Holder, having gathered everything, emanated it into the bhaga of the queen, the blissful one; there the maṇḍala should be drawn." This teaches the reality of the other just as it is. The intermediate is the reality of oneself, in which Vajradhara gathers all the cakras into his own center and makes them the nature of all vajras. To meditate by that rite is the supreme.

In "the illusion of all women," "all women" are form and so forth. "Illusion" is quality, abiding by nature as body and so forth. "Illusion": "without remainder, through the illusion of qualities," is said in the sense of illusion. "Without remainder" means the parts, that is, having parts.

"The part is taught as the thing;
the partless is what is called quality.
Quality joined with the possessor of quality,
object and sense faculty, are wholly virtuous.
The vowels and consonants are declared to be the qualities;
sameness is the domain of the samaya."

Since that very thing is a sign, it is the mudrā. "This" means made manifest by the mind of the apprehender. The very mode of nonduality of the long and so forth, which through that quality always possess quality, is "supreme," meaning unsurpassed and sacred. That is because it is accomplished by nature as excelling others, such as cast images. "Accomplished by nature" means having the characteristic of changelessness and a pure essential nature, just as curd and the like are in milk. Therefore it is distinct from what is contrived.

Further, regarding the words "Even those of bad conduct will attain accomplishment through gain, bliss, and all joy": "gain" is gaining the awakening mind, and that comes about because it increases especially through all joys. Living by begging and the like, and the practices of austerity and so forth, belong to the opposing side, so they never lead to accomplishment and are to be abandoned.

"By that" means by the substance of accomplishment. If one asks of what particular kind, it is accomplished by nature and by essential nature: from the transformation of the syllables of the nature mantra, there is complete transformation into the form of one's own deity, which is mantra recitation; and from the transformation of one's own seed, there is complete transformation of the nature.

"Various" means the nature of the elements, divided into eighteen kinds of ḍākinīs, and not the worlds such as Bhuvaḥ.

In "accomplished in roaming throughout the sky," the word "sky" refers to the mind, because just as the space element is the ground of everything, the mind gives room to everything. Or else it is "sky" because the abodes of the śrāvakas and the like have been burned away; and because she roams and acts in that sky with the nature of nectar, it says: "Since she is accomplished in roaming throughout the sky, she is renowned as a ḍākinī."

In "the various, universal mudrā," "universal" means all the vajra families of body, speech, and mind. "Various" is the great Vajradhara, and his mudrās are all the mudrās of the circle of the maṇḍala. Therefore it is "the various mudrā." Through the nature of mind, those substances of accomplishment, the moon and the red, become powerful by being constantly cultivated up to twenty-five.

"Various vow" means accomplishing bliss of various kinds. "All ḍākas" refers to the rite of union with blood: by that rite the mantrin becomes renowned as Vajraḍāka, meaning that he himself has come to be born as Vajraḍāka. "Mind without reality" means being without the mind of suchness, that is, applying oneself with a mistaken mind. "Focusing on the deity" means meditating on oneself as the deity one desires. "Utterly the mind of reality" means being adorned with the mind of suchness; its defining mark is that it is unmistaken.

Furthermore, the elements such as blood are the suchness of the intrinsic nature of the buddhas, the ground of all accomplishments. "The yoga of cast images and the like" means abandoning that and meditating on something artificial, because it is not even a reflected image. This teaches the following: just as someone might throw away the ripened fruit and take the root of the tree instead, so too one who throws away the genuine and clings to the artificial shows himself to be of inferior intellect and utterly foolish.

Therefore, when one joins oneself to the perfection of the mind of suchness and, through the completion stage, focuses on the body of a buddha, one accomplishes the supremely unchanging wisdom. That very wisdom is supremely unchanging because it has neither beginning nor end. Therefore, moreover, abandoning reflected images, cast images and the like, one should regard the very cognition of the various elements as the gathering of all the buddhas.

In the lines "At the time of deity yoga, from the pores of the body hairs," the body hairs are the navel maṇḍala, and the pores are space. "As far as the tip" refers to the elements transformed from the sixteen letters. The emanation is the samaya maṇḍala:

The yogin should always meditate on it.
What is called the samaya maṇḍala
Is hūṃ resting upon a moon
In the center of one's own navel lotus.
Thousands of ten millions of buddhas
It emanates into the ten directions.
In the navel maṇḍala of sixteen parts,
Laid out with lines by the mantrin
Using the cord for the sixteen drops,
Are a and the rest, ending with kṣa.
From the last the mantra should be extracted.
Afterward, in the drop, it is free of parts.
Since it has the nature of feces and urine,
Here kṣa is to be left aside.
Through the transformation of the letter ha,
All the letters are like branches.
This is the self-arisen Blessed One,
The sole one, the supreme deity.
The emptiness that is the reality of all buddhas,
The letter itself, is the ḍākinī.

This is the pith instruction on the mantra.

The letters, well set out, beginning with ka,
Are the forms of the deities.
Graceful and adorned with the mantra
Of all the vowels beginning with a,
The supreme deity, the letter hūṃ,
Should be imagined at the center of the navel.
The navel is called the lotus;
This contemplation is to be cultivated.
The emptiness that is the reality of all buddhas
Is the very form of consciousness, hūṃ.
The letter itself is the ḍākinī,
Abiding in the form of sound.
Because, moreover, these
Are just as all the buddhas are,
This is to be recited always,
And it abides in the heart of all the buddhas.
Free of head and of drop,
The sole one, the supreme deity,
Abiding at one's own center, the Vajra Holder,
Is surrounded by the letters.

In the phrase "Entering the sublime domain of the light of suchness," wisdom is the full knowing of the sphere of experience. "Full knowing" is the wisdom that knows everything; by that very wisdom, oneself is all the buddhas. "Oneself" is the conventional, and fully knowing the conventional is buddhahood itself. By that very wisdom one is accomplished as a buddha and as Vajradhara. If one asks by what means, the answer runs up to the words "the light of suchness."

Union with all the ḍākas,
Vajraḍāka, the supreme bliss:
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken it.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the forty-second chapter, setting out the stages of the arising of suchness, beginning with "the secret."

The rite of the fire offering activity
I shall explain just as it is; listen, O Goddess!
For the sake of kingship the mantrin
Should recite ten thousand times.
Seated upon a messenger,
He should perform five thousand fire offerings.
If, in this sequence, he burns
Great flesh mixed with milk,
He will obtain palaces
Within a range of up to ten million leagues.
The glorious great kingdom thus accomplished
Can be destroyed by no one.

With the flesh of humans, cattle and jackals
There will likewise be the most wondrous results:
Soaked in liquor and in milk,
If one performs a hundred thousand fire offerings,
One gains the great glory of kingship and its riches,
Holding sway over the seven continents.

Next, whoever wishes to destroy
One who commits the five deeds of immediate retribution
Should take poison, blood and black oil,
And burnt human bones,
With thorny wood,
And chaff as well, along with human hair,
And kindle a fire taken from a butcher's house.
Naked, with hair hanging loose,
Facing toward the south,
Filled with wrath, at midday,
With undistracted mind, he should burn them;
Or else, wearing blue garments,
He should burn them at midnight.
At the end of the mantra, join the name.
If even a powerful enemy
Is destroyed together with his army,
What need is there to speak of an ordinary person?

Next, the rite is explained for driving out
An enemy made arrogant by his power.
Crow wings and fat,
Mixed with the juice of nimba,
Burned in a fire kindled with piśāca wood
While facing toward the wind:
Within seven nights at most
He will be driven out; of this there is no doubt.

Next, I shall explain sowing discord.
In a blazing fire of datura,
Mix nimba leaves
With snakeskin,
Together with wood
From the nests of crows and owls.
If one burns these a hundred and eight times,
The target, along with friends and kin,
Will be hated by all people.

Next, attraction is to be explained.
Visualize the target on a wooden seat,
Sporting and swaying,
Face turned upward by the wind,
Of a color like vermilion.
Through the application of noose and hook,
Recite ja at the end of the mantra,
And pierce the lotus of the target's heart;
If it is a woman, pierce her womb.
There is no doubt one will win the three realms.

Next, another rite is explained,
Whereby attraction and the rest come about as intended.
A skull of a single piece,
Beautifully formed, unbroken and clean:
Draw on it the form of the target.
On a hundred fresh leaves,
With blood and gorocanā,
Using a pen of datura,
Write the mantra together with the name.
Place them inside the skull,
And cover the target with red cloth.
Swelling with the pride of the mantra deity,
Take up the leaves one by one,
Recite the secret heart mantra in reverse order,
And burn all the leaves.
The target is summoned just as one has in mind.

Next, another: from red sandalwood
Make the form of the target.
With one's own blood and gorocanā
Write the name and insert it at the target's heart.
Smear it with the three hot spices,
And if one pierces the heart, the navel and the secret place
With a copper needle,
At midnight the target is summoned as one wishes.

With red ochre draw
The shape of a vulva; upon it
Place the left hand,
And if one recites a hundred and eight times,
Whoever's name one has taken
Will come at once.

On a leaf tossed about by the wind,
With a pen made from a crow's feather,
Write a yantra together with the name
Using tears,
And send it off on a whirlwind rising upward.
By that rite the target is driven out.

A ritual dagger of monkey bone
Should be made six finger-widths in length.
Having recited the mantra seven times over it,
If one hides it at the door of someone's house,
His family line will be cut off.
If one hides it where cattle, horses, elephants,
Donkeys, camels or buffalo are kept,
They will perish.

Visualize the target, dwelling in the wind maṇḍala,
Mounted on a camel,
Naked and smeared with melted butter,
Hair unbound, facing south,
Helpless, weeping and fainting.
His neck is bound with a noose,
And from behind a wrathful one strikes him.
With the intent of subduing the three realms,
Face toward the southeast, the direction of fire,
And recite ja at the end of the mantra:
It will be accomplished exactly as one wishes.

One should meditate on the enemies,
Mounted on horses and on buffalo,
Turning on one another,
Weapons in hand, enraged,
Fighting one another
Together with their armies.
The mantra practitioner, facing the battle,
Should write the syllable ra
Interspersed with the target's name,
And imagine the target's face turned upward.
The practitioner, upon a corpse,
Sits unmoving in the vajra posture;
Pressing the target down with it,
He should torment his body and speech.
Interspersing the mantra with the name,
He should recite it as before.
This is the rite of binding.

United with all the ḍākas,
Vajraḍāka, the supreme bliss,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata:
Thus it was spoken by the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

This is the forty-third chapter of the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, setting out in order the arising of the meditative concentrations for all rites and the procedures of the fire offering.

Now I shall fully explain
Another rite of fire offering: listen, goddess!
A coquettish woman, or any other,
Without blemish and exceedingly beautiful,
Found on the day of Bhūta:
If the practitioner accomplishes her as explained before,
All accomplishments will be perfectly achieved.
A cloth used to wipe away menstrual blood,
Together with cat dung,
Finely ground, should be made into incense.
With this incense the practitioner

will be greatly blessed by this incense.
From the milk of a black bitch
and black gram together with fruit,
prepare a milk porridge, and in the mouth of a corpse
perform the fire offering without fear.
Then that corpse will rise
and say, "What shall I do?"
Pills, the accomplishment of eye ointment,
foot ointment, and likewise the sword,
treasure, the destruction of enemies,
yakṣiṇīs, and the elixir of rejuvenation:
having granted the one thing requested,
it will depart, and not otherwise.
One will sport with the heroes;
I do not speak of this elsewhere.

Next I shall explain another
means of accomplishing the great vetāla.
Taking a beautiful corpse
having the characteristics taught before,
well established in the great yoga,
one meditates at midnight.
If one performs a hundred and eight fire offerings
with its flesh together with the great red,
the glorious Vajravārāhī,
surrounded by ḍākinīs
and encircled by many vetālas and rākṣasas,
terrifying with her laughter, "Ha ha!",
will come there into one's presence.
Many hundreds of jackals
will surround her and raise terrifying cries.
Seeing this, one should not be afraid,
but make one's body firm with mantra
and think of oneself as the vajra discipline.
"Kye ho! Great hero, excellent!"
Having spoken thus, the goddess
will bestow even a hundred accomplishments,
whatever the practitioner wishes in his mind.
Surrounded by yoginīs,
the hero goes to his own abode.
In that very instant
he takes on a divine body
and will roam through all three realms.
The hero has whatever form he wishes;
fortunate, he is like a tathāgata.
He constantly flies through the sky
and is wholly free of aging and death.
He will roam over all the earth,
and accomplishments come just as he wishes.

Then, in order to pacify the afflictions
and to increase meditative absorption,
abiding in the yoga of one's own deity,
one should begin the inner fire offering.
In the center of a moon maṇḍala
one should constantly imagine oneself.
With garlands of light rays, turbulent and fierce,
and garlands of maṇḍalas of light,
the Victors dwelling in the ten directions,
equal in number to the atoms of a great trichiliocosm,
white and of the other colors, are drawn in
and should be made to enter that supreme body.
This is the fire offering of pacifying.
For increasing, summon the bodhisattvas
and intently imagine them in rigid form.
In order utterly to exhaust the afflictions,
one does it especially in wrathful form.
In order to attract many kinds of beings,
imagine them in the form of women.
This, as the inner fire offering,
is taught to all practitioners.
The fuel, the aggregates and the rest,
set in motion by the wind of karma,
should be burned in the fire of wisdom
that blazes from the navel maṇḍala.
The fire of Brahmā abides at the waist.
The lalanā is the filling ladle;
the rasanā is the mouth of the pouring ladle.
The handle runs from below the vajra jewel
to the tip of one's own vajra.
The skull cup is the vessel for the burnt substances,
and all substances should be burned.
This is the very procedure of the rite
of the unsurpassed fire offering;
it is explained to you, O goddess.
If one does this, meditative absorption
will not decline but become stable.
This rite is well known
as common to all the tantras.

Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
united with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken it.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the forty-fourth chapter, setting out the accomplishment of the vetāla and the procedure of the rite of the inner fire offering.

Listen, goddess, and I shall fully explain
the counting rosary according to its rite,
the correct rite through which
it will be accomplished, without doubt.
Crystal, mother-of-pearl, pearl,
and all minerals and the like, or others too, will serve.
Conch, lungtang seeds, and lapis
increase life span and health.
Gold and likewise silver,
and especially lotus seeds:
if the wise one counts on a beautiful rosary of jewels
and recites mantra for the sake of kingship,
he will swiftly obtain great glory.
From saffron and other fragrances,
and especially from coral,
red sandalwood, silver, and the like,
the practitioner should make the beads.
These are supreme for attachment and passion
and bestow the result upon the practitioner.
Rudrākṣa, lungtang seeds,
and likewise human bone
should be applied to fierce actions,
and especially to sorcery.
For pacifying, increasing, magnetizing, and sorcery,
putraṃjīva accomplishes every action.
For pleasing the mantra there are fifty;
for passion, half of that;
for pacifying there are a hundred;
for increasing, add eight more;
for sorcery there are sixty:
apply them according to the type of action.
According to the type of action,
the counting rosaries should be made.
One should string them on a golden thread;
that is virtuous and accomplishes all actions.
Or else a cotton thread
spun by a young maiden, beautiful in form,
with limbs entirely complete,
intelligent and gentle in speech:
string them on the thread she has spun,
and the accomplishment of the mantra will be released.
The beads are arhats,
and the top bead should be regarded as a stupa.
The stupa being the lord of Dharma,
the dharmadhātu should be placed in the center.
For expelling, use crow feathers
and the hair of donkeys and camels.
With the hair of horses and buffaloes
make the thread for mutual enmity.
Thread of hair from the charnel ground,
mixed with dog hair, should be spun;
this is taught in brief
especially for fierce actions.
For pacifying, place it on the index finger;
for increasing, move it with the middle finger;
for magnetizing, the ring finger is taught;
with the last, perform sorcery.
Making the thumb into a hook,
imagine summoning all the deities.
If one recites mantra in deep equipoise,
one will succeed; of this there is no doubt.

Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
united with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken it.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the forty-fifth chapter, setting out the procedure of the rite of mantra recitation.

Then, with the vajra inserted into the lotus, moving and dancing, the goddess spoke these words to the Blessed One:

"Temples and mansions,
stupas, maṇḍalas, cities,
houses, gardens and the like;
digging into the belly of the mahoraga;
the disciple's taking possession of the ground;
the preparation of the vase;
the rite of protection that comes first;
the preparation of the deities and the disciples;
the wisdom thread and the colored powders:
please explain these in brief."

"I shall explain the rites of the disciple
just as they are. Goddess, listen!
To one who has the characteristics described before,
whose body is perfected in wisdom,
with a mind intent on the welfare of sentient beings,
one should make supplication with every kind of possession.
Then, for the sake of drawing the maṇḍala,
the disciple should request the guru
again and again.
When he has been asked, the guru should say,
'Have you obtained all the requisites?'
If the disciple says,
'A few I have obtained,
but some I have not obtained,'
then the great master
should examine his mind.
He should know the steady as steady,
the wavering as wavering,
and the one who must be led as one who must be led.
At the soles of the disciple's feet
imagine a bow-shaped wind maṇḍala;
at the navel, the blazing fire of wisdom;
its rays blaze at the heart;
meditate that by their blazing they billow forth everywhere.
The ugly form arising from vaṃ,
which is his sin, is expelled through the nostrils.
Then meditate on the form of a wrathful one,
and meditate that it devours it entirely.
oṃ vajraḍākinī kha kha khāhi khāhi
sarva pāpaṃ daha daha bhasmiṃ kuru hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ
Having generated the one to be accomplished in the form of the deity,
one should set the protection in place.
Who is able to examine
the maṇḍala according to its rite?
If one examines the maṇḍala
through the yoga of one's own deity, it will succeed.
Of a village, a charnel ground, a marketplace,
a grove, a temple or a city,
the ground to the east and north is auspicious;
the south and west are not so.
The living quarters of a monastery,
a grove, a mountain peak, a flat rock,
the roof of a mansion,
a charnel ground and the house of a god:
all such portions of ground
are praised in all the tantras.
One should know the characteristics of the ground.
Or else, wherever the mind delights,
draw the maṇḍala in those places.
According to the kind of accomplishment, lesser and the rest,
dig to the depth of the knee, the hip or the navel,
remove husks, stones,
potsherds, charcoal, splinters,
thorns and the like, and tamp it down.
Afterwards dig a hole a span deep,
fill it completely with water,
face east and walk a hundred paces;
on returning, examine the result.
If the water remains full, it is auspicious;
if it has dried up, the mantrin should abandon the site.
First put in grains and so on.
Having thus purified the portion of ground,
visualize, arising from the seed syllable laṃ,
the earth goddess Pṛthivī, adorned with garments and ornaments,
with one face and two arms,
blazing all around with yellow light,
holding in her hand
a golden vase,
abiding with half her body within the earth.
Looking at her, with the right hand
touching the ground, one should urge her:
oṃ ehyehi mahādevi pṛthvī lokamātare
sarvva ratna saṃbūrṇṇa divya alaṅkāra bhūṣite hāra nūbura nirghoṣa vajrasatva prapūjite gṛhītvā
idaṃ arghaṃ asmin sannihito bhava hrīḥ hi hi hi hi svāhā
Having worshipped her with flowers, scents and the like,
imagine that she dissolves into that very place.
On a mansion, on a base of flat rock,
in a temple, at a place trodden by holy feet,
in a cattle shed or on the shore of the ocean,
having placed the hand on the ground,
recite one's own heart mantra eight hundred times;
there, digging and the rest are not needed.
On ground purified in this way,
in accordance with the maṇḍala,
make a fourfold maṇḍala house
of jewels and the like.
Reciting oṃ āḥ laṃ hūṃ,
make what lies beneath the ground into vajra substance.
oṃ medini vajri bhava hūṃ:
with this sequence fill the hole.
In its center, meditate on the great wrathful one
blazing like the fire at the end of the eon.
Then emanate the hawk-faced one and the others,
above in the form of wrathful goddesses
and from the navel down single-pointed,
in the form of vajras.
Gazing downward at the host of obstructors,
with a haughty bearing, reciting the mantra,
drive them into their hearts.
oṃ vajraḍākinī sarvva vighnān anucchedaya kīlaya hūṃ phaṭ
Then, in the center of the ground described before,
through the yoga of the great Vajradhara,
abiding as oneself,
meditate that it is equal to space.
This is the view of the ultimate;
this is the supreme and sacred protection.
Whatever rites of protection there are
are all contained in this very one.
Then make a net of vajra arrows,
a vajra fence,
and a ground of vajra nature.
Having spread out the great royal enclosure,
say hūṃ over the ground of the maṇḍala.
Circle it all around with the vajra gait,
and anoint it all over with the five nectars.
At the places of the Tathāgatas
make squares with sandalwood and the like;
at the places of the ordinary deities
make circles for each;
then, following the rite of the deity wheel,
worship with flowers and the like.
Then, of precious gold or silver,
copper, or even clay,
in keeping with one's means,
the vases should be made.
Avoid those with a black base and the like;
the belly too should have the shape of a fine vase.
With vyāghrī, siṃhī, daṇḍotpala
and aparājitā,
together with the great medicine sahadevā;
gold, silver, pearl,
sapphire and coral, the five jewels;
sesame, barley, wheat,
peas and lentils together with mung beans;
camphor, red sandalwood and the like:
fill them with water containing these medicines.
In this way provide them with the flowers and water
of the great samaya.
Because things are pure by intrinsic nature,
do not judge them pleasant or unpleasant.
Since all things arise from conceptual thought,
do not discriminate between pleasant and unpleasant.
The power of substances is inconceivable;
it brings one down into buddhahood itself.
For example, the root of the spa cane,
white or black, smeared
on the ears and the forehead,
stops a woman's menstrual flow.
Just as elephant urine, gabakṣa
and white seeds, smeared on the eyes,
purge the belly,
so here too, by making use of special requisites
that have been purified,
one attains a special result.
For example, poison purified by mantra,
when eaten, destroys
all diseases and becomes an elixir.
In the same way one should examine this here too.
Over all of them, with their respective emblems
and their individual mantras, recite.
Make the emblems in their respective colors.
Wrap the neck with a new pair of cloths,
cover the mouth with fresh leaves,
spread a bundle of kuśa grass beneath,
and adorn it with dyed thread.
Having performed the preparatory consecration and worship,
and having offered incense,

It should be placed in the center of the wheel.
Then, setting the right knee
Upon the ground and touching the crown of the head to it,
One should speak these words and ask to be heeded:
"O maṇḍala, whose nature is compassion,
Out of love for the disciples
And in order to worship you as well,
Today I wish to draw it.
To fulfill all hopes as they are
And for the benefit of sentient beings,
Protector, upon me who am devoted
Please bestow your kindness."
This should be recited three times.
Then, having completed the preparation of the vase:
"Buddhas, protectors of the world,
Today regard me with love.
Bodhisattvas and arhats,
And whatever other mantra deities there are,
Gods who protect the world,
Bhūtas of great miraculous power
Who delight in the teaching,
And all who possess the divine eye:
I, the vajra holder named so-and-so,
Today wish to draw, with whatever implements I have,
The virtuous maṇḍala called such-and-such.
Upon the maṇḍala,
Upon me and upon the assembly of disciples,
May all of you be present and bestow your blessing."
According to the rite, this is recited three times.

Then, having twisted fresh thread well,
With one of proper measure and great beauty,
The wise one should snap the lines with the thread.
According to the rite, flowers are offered.
The thread, divided into twenty-five strands,
Should be stretched out.
East and west, north and south:
By this sequence of stretching the line cord
The maṇḍala is divided into eight parts.
Gathering the remaining half in the hand,
One should begin the rite of snapping the lines.
The left vajra fist
Should be placed and held at the navel;
Below that, do as is comfortable.
The mantrin, facing east,
Visualizing all directions as even,
Should draw the two Brahmā lines,
Even and of proper measure.
Standing at the fire corner (southeast), toward the wind corner (northwest),
And standing at the corner of Nairṛti, toward Īśāna,
The mantrin, facing east and south,
Should snap the threads.
Here master and disciple
Should circle to the right.
If there is no disciple, one should oneself
Tie it to a peg and draw.
Having doubled it, fix it to a peg.
The inner wheel, the vajra fence,
And the maṇḍala of light, in two layers:
Beginning from the corner of Īśāna,
Wind the thread clockwise.
The door is one eighth of the maṇḍala;
It is held to be without a rim.
The door recess is the measure of the door,
And with half of that the side walls are made.
The archway (toraṇa) is three times the door,
Furnished with banners and bells.
With victory banners, flags and deer,
The portico should be made of proper measure.
Outside the archway,
At twice the colored ground,
Make the outer maṇḍala of light.
Outside that wheel
Make the charnel grounds with trees and the like.
Further, in keeping with the wheel,
Consider carefully how it may be beautiful.
If the line cord breaks, the master dies;
If too short or too long, illness arises;
If the direction is mistaken, the disciple goes mad.
Therefore one should know it to be flawless.
Having requested the wisdom line cord to depart,
Then one should begin this work.
One should prepare the colored powders
That bestow both desires and liberation:
White, yellow, red and green,
And within them all, black.
The portions of color are laid down in order;
Then the disciple may lay them down as he pleases.
The width of each line of colored powder
Is one twentieth of the door.
If the colored powders are uneven,
Crooked or low,
Because the drawing is low the purpose is not achieved,
And because crookedness produces torment,
They should be drawn high and straight,
About a little finger's breadth in height.
If thicker than that, illness arises;
If low, wealth is exhausted;
If crooked, there is mutual hatred;
If broken, know that both guru and disciple will die.
If it is drawn other than clockwise,
The colored powder becomes as if struck with kīla daggers.
The east is made a very white color,
The south is made red,
The west is made yellow,
And the north is made green.
Through the rite and these,
The seats of the four buddhas,
The heroes and yoginīs beginning with Vajra,
Ḍākinī and the others on the petals,
And Kākāsyā and the rest, should be marked out.
In the center, a variegated lotus
Endowed with filaments;
In the corners, skull cups.
White, red and likewise blue:
The colors of the bodies and so forth are distinguished.
Thus, great goddess, the wheel
And the heroes should be worshipped.
On ground with the characteristics explained before,
It should be drawn high in the center.
Ground sloping to the east and north
Brings accomplishment to mantrins.
Through height in the center, kingship
And the abodes of the vidyādharas are attained.
Through height in the north, one dies,
Or one's wealth is lost,
Or else one is struck by illness.
Through height in the east, as has been said,
The families are swiftly exhausted.
On ground whose nature is low in the center,
The practitioner meets with destruction.
In the Tattvasaṃgraha, the Cakrasaṃvara,
The Guhyasamāja and the Vajrabhairava,
This king of maṇḍalas
Has not appeared and will not appear.
Whatever has been explained or not explained,
All of it abides in Heruka.
Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
In union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken it.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the forty-sixth chapter, on the sequence of rites for taking possession of the ground, the preparation, and the drawing of the lines.

Then the goddess, having worshipped the Blessed One and bowed to him, said this:

In the seventeenth chapter
you taught the elixir of the constituents.
Into how many kinds is the outer divided,
and into how many kinds the inner?
Enemy, friend and neutral,
and likewise the further divisions of measure,
dry ground and moist country,
the collected classes of the constituents:
about these great wonders
doubt has arisen in me.

The Blessed One declared:
Now I shall fully explain
the classes and their connections; listen.
By the combination of gross and subtle,
the distinctions of small and so on,
wind, bile, phlegm and the rest,
males and females as well,
each is held to be threefold.
Black, tawny and pale blue,
rough and smooth and firm of limb,
she is called "the one with moles."
Stout, short, of middle height and tall;
pale blue, even, rough, long-necked,
with eyes red like copper,
garlanded, common, without breasts.
Of faded complexion, with the scent of śāli rice,
with the scent of liquor, and an intoxicating scent not of liquor:
"two liquors" and "three liquors,"
these are the distinctions of the waist.
Flowing like honey, with the scent of honey:
these are to be dealt with separately, not otherwise.
By the device of mental attention
the girl's essence is drawn out and poured.
This is the separate practice.
One girl, two,
and up to many should be examined.
When their qualities have become clear,
they produce the qualities of the practitioner's faculties.
Up to twenty they are youthful;
they should be purified through skillful means.
The place to be taken as possessing strength,
and what comes from embracing her,
is what purifies the substances.
That which holds the boundary of every act
is the great path of agent and eater.
The great tree of mercury;
therefore the water-born has five qualities:
it destroys its own faults and is trustworthy.
These are the common rites.

The work of the substances, the belly-born not grown old;
the aim of the generative is to destroy old age.
Mica is supreme for binding mercury.
Through the rite of the great gold-transmuting elixir
magical powers and the like become manifest.
Mica and mercury are of four kinds:
with the form of a peacock's neck,
the form of a snake, the form of a frog,
and following the appearance of a great cloud.
Their form, sound, smell and taste,
and their measures, should all be known.
And if the potent one lacks its essence,
various faults arise as before.
Mercury and mica
with mixed colors are faulty:
they give rise to faults of phlegm
and of bile as well.
The red gives rise to disorders of the blood;
the white gives rise to stabbing pains
and destroys life.
The yellow-green leaves things unripened,
and the tawny blocks the passages of feces and urine.
A double portion binds severely.
Since there are these eight faults,
the intelligent should examine before consuming.
Having come to know the blue elixir,
with effort practice the elixir in this way.

For setting it down: in a place of fire,
leaning against a plantain tree,
setting it in the midst of grain,
and placing the apparatus in a heap of sand.
For nourishing it, some use fruits,
some flowers with their petals,
bark, bones and the like.
The articles close at hand for the practitioner
should be applied by one who knows reality.
One who has seen many rites,
if he obtains the requisites,
whether prepared or natural,
becomes equal to Vajradhara.
This is the rite of setting down and nourishing.

In dung, inside stone,
in cattle and buffalo sheds and the like,
examined according to the rite,
the substance of the essence should be set down.
Having come to know the blue elixir,
with effort perform the elixir in this way.
This is the rite of the place for examining the essence.

Pouring the essence into an elephant's head
and cooking it in a lion,
it gains the quality of yielding cooked rice.
Or else, another means of nourishing:
it should be nourished with the root of śirīṣa
and with gold leaf.
Red-colored mercury, by stupefying it
with skins and barks,
with flowers and fruits,
cooked together with nonpo,
when kneaded, scatters the dust of its impurities.
With an unerring understanding
knead it step by step.
These are the rites of insertion.

Then, with salt of urine,
pig's grease, fat and blood,
kneading it with these also removes its faults.
Again, bhūta hair,
janita and red matted hair,
binjara, campa
and thorn apple, in equal parts,
kneaded into an equal amount of honey,
exhaust the faults of the elixir.
Then into a buffalo's hoof
together with rainwater,
pour copper powder, bronze powder
and eye-salve powder, and cook it.
When cooked, place it in honey;
by this it becomes bound.
Then, if it is purified with seven parts of blood,
its oiliness, heaviness and so on,
its strength, become exhausted.
Again, kneaded with an equal part of blood,
it becomes oily and heavy.
Brass and mango wood,
copper, turmeric,
and gorasa, both white and black:
these six cut off the faults.

Then candra wood,
honey drip and nail,
alkali, powder of worm-eaten wood,
mahāhahā and makṣa,
puṣkara and bartaki,
and powder of kuba sindhuvara:
put these together into a human bone.
Leave it a little while, then take it out,
and pound it in a stone mortar as explained before.
Pour it into a bamboo tube,
then put it into the apparatus.
When cooked, together with mica and silver powder,
pour it into the elixir.
By the rite of the sharp mica and mercury
bind them each separately.
Those that come from the moon,
the bone of atrapata,
and likewise take flowers:
vyāghrī and kunduru,
sāriva and nail,
the moonlight jewel and nāgakesara,
emblic myrobalan, rakhu, kadamba,
campaka, kirisopa,
shingsho, bilva, utpala,
together with sour juice,
the honey drip and the mercury are to be dyed crow-black.
When taken out, it becomes like the form of a snake.
Pour in blood, thorn apple and turmeric;
it destroys all faults, such as white hair.
Boiling butter and black butter,
both should be done again and again.
With plantain, apāmārga,
snuhī, palāśa leaves,
the essence of sopañcana,
and the group of alkalis, it should be purified.
This is the rite of purification.

Then sour gruel and turmeric,
and sharp substances, are given as food to the mercury.
Then, by the downward route,
the cleansing treatment is done with the apparatus.
It removes old age and leprosy.
With sharp substances together with alkali,
when washed and anointed, it destroys hemorrhoids.
The elixir and rejuvenation increase step by step.
Relying on sharp substances and on mica
together in common,
it is tamed by pouring in lead,
and then its taste is bitter and astringent.
Then, within sour gruel,
if it is cooked with cow dung, its faults are removed.
Even warming it in the light of the sun
produces the fault of white hair.
The vapor of an essence boiled too much
destroys the heart and the eyes.
For this, it is purified
by giving drops of honey and posoncha.
Before the rite of purifying faults,
prepare it with gold leaf, alkali and salt.
Pour in a paste of white mica
that has an astringent taste.
If it is kneaded for a long time
with fixed proportions,
the faults are removed, and not otherwise.
Furthermore, the part and that which has parts,
powder of girikarṇa,
and a sixth part is to be mercury.
A measured proportion surely binds it.
Then, by the inverted route,
pour it in according to the rite of the apparatus,
and cook it in the zhigajé.
Then turuṣka,
the seed of pālicakramarda,
palataka,
the fruit of kaśiśigru,
the flower of baricitaka,
utpala and mālikā seeds,
the kernels of juniper,
śirīṣa and kuśa seeds,
honey and posoncha,
and the bark of palāśa:
one who knows the rite
should cook these in the zhigajé and pour them in well.
Then, having taken it out according to the rite,
grind them again,
and pour in the powder and the paste.

Cooking, burning,
killing, and pressing down.
For cooking there are the skull cup apparatus,
the bamboo, and the shape of a crow's beak.
Hiding, raging, scattering,
and fleeing into water.
The signs of its being thoroughly cooked
are trembling, stupor,
swirling into smoke, and giving off a hissing sound.
Squeezing, kneading, stirring,
and pounding are the four kinds.
Cooking in honey and cooking in the bee,
cooking in the elephant and cooking in the lion,
the mare and the karan,
the donkey, and the camel's mouth:
for binding and for killing
these apparatuses are highly praised.
Whatever apparatus has a wide mouth,
shaped like a clay drum,
like a bamboo and so forth,
like a weapon or a sword's sheath,
the heron's neck, the crow's beak,
and whatever else is taught for binding:
with these one should kill and foster.
Boiled until a quarter remains is the great decoction;
boiled until half remains is the potent decoction.
The faults that rest on the elixir
are removed by that uncommon one.
Purifying, awakening,
stupefying, and likewise killing.
Into the bound mica medicine
one should pour silver powder.

One hero, two twice-born roots,
three great astringent fruits,
four, mica and mercury,
five essences of nectar,
six seasons, and likewise sixty;
the seven, with branches, leaves and so on.
Hot and bitter ones grown in dry ground are best;
sweet and sour ones from moist ground are good;
for salty and astringent, either kind will do.
Nimba, beleric,
and the fruits of myrobalan,
if boiled in sesame oil, do their work.
Emblic juice removes bile,
and boiled in a woman's milk
it sustains life and increases health.
Campaka and bakula,
nāgakesara, kumuda,
utpala and śephālikā,
honey-drip and priyaṅgu,
kusumbha and spṛkkā,
and reṇukā make eleven:
these are called the group of flowers.
Śrīphala, posoncha,
kapittha and lakuca,
kuba and vārtākī,
myrobalan and beleric,
palataka, emblic,
elaji and punaka,
castor plant and marica,
and pippali make sixteen:
these are the group of fruits.
Chawa and sweet flag,
turmeric and costus,
chalga and garlic,
the doba of moist ground,
white nāgapāla,
likewise the one that makes the old young,
bālika, and the beautiful night tree:
these twelve are the group of roots,
which grant every accomplishment.

Elephant hair and mountain beauty,
ooze, and bālaka and the dense one,
red matted-hair, dhattūra,
and aśvagandha: these seven,
with trunk, branches and roots.
Kadamba and the golden tree,
and likewise śirīṣa,
karañja and śigru,
nimba and pilasi,
and likewise kaṭu and mañjarī:
these eight are called the bark group.
The two sandalwoods and aguru,
with the black, make three: take their trunks.
Molasses, powdered sugar,
honey, ikṣu, puṣkara
and śarkarā, these six kinds,
are called the essence group.
Cuckoo and crow,
peacock and the thibya bird:
take the flesh of these;
it increases vigor and strength.
Rooster, gharkara,
pigeon and parrot:
take the broth of these;
it gives great strength to all beings.
Goat, hare and deer,
porcupine and pig,
and especially rats
destroy all diseases;
they are especially praised for wind disorders.
Riunuka and barthiba,
mango and bilva flowers,
mañjarī and the enemy of the asuras,
surasi and the matted one,
paparnaga and puṣpa,
arjaka leaves,
kaṭu and piśana,
lamp-tree and vidyādhara,
and the branch explained as fivefold:
these are called the friend group.
Parpari and gana,
jala and gyagyu,
the bark of upita,
pus-smell and thief,
khutra, loha, śukati,
water-born tree and rock exudate,
honey-flower, bhṛṅgara,
hata and śepali,
and likewise payasi:
this is the middling group.
Dirada and myrrh,
curd, kunduru, kadaja,
pine and lac,
the juice of posonchaya,
calamus and gné,
tuhina and the goat-born,
mṛga and baguja,
white garlic and misi,
asafoetida and turmeric:
these are explained as the enemy group;
they pervade the enemy substances.
Guggulu and vṛṣa,
kitang and likewise milk,
borax and vinegar,
camphor and salaki:
these nine are the light substances.
Mura, red matted-hair,
jala and gyagyu,
the dense one, costus and kuthira,
niśa and śigru,
śira, gcawa and calamus:
these twelve are born from roots.
Puthi and keso,
musk and nails,
and what is called gandakuri:
these five, which come from animals,
the practitioner should know as fragrances.
Śirīṣa and the golden tree,
lodhra and kapittha,
nāgika and picu bark,
and what is called the great sour essence:
these seven are the bark group.
The three sandalwoods and black aguru,
lamp-tree and śiṃśapā:
these six are everywhere explained
as the group of stems.

Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
renowned as Vajraḍāka,
who joins in union with all illusions,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, spoke this.

This is the forty-seventh chapter of the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, the section on the ritual procedure concerning the substances for essence extraction.

Then the goddess made offerings to the Blessed One, bowed, and spoke these words:

"Though you have earlier taught the fire offering
that accomplishes all activities,
I do not know the measurements of the hearths,
the posture, the kindling sticks, and the rest."

The Blessed One declared:

"Listen, goddess Mahāmāyā,
who sports through all illusions!
First, having taken possession of the ground,
afterward one should begin the homa pit.
For pacifying, on a white ground,
make it perfectly round, a cubit in measure,
and dig it half a cubit deep.
Resting in perfect meditative equipoise,
mark it by drawing an eight-finger vajra
upon a lotus.
Leaving four fingers from the pit,
surround it with a rim
four fingers high and wide.
On its border, surround it with
three-pronged vajras of vajra shape.
With white earth and cow urine
smear the homa pit all over.
The one who keeps the vow, having anointed both
with white perfume,
resting in perfect meditative equipoise,
should scatter white flowers.
Begin that rite in the eastern direction
of the maṇḍala house and so forth.
For enriching, on a yellow ground,
make a hearth shaped like a jewel,
or else it may be square;
two cubits wide, dig it a cubit deep.
Leaving eight fingers inside it,
make the rim eight fingers in measure;
on it, eight fingers high,
surround it with a garland of jewels.
Inside the homa pit draw
a yellow wheel of eight spokes,
and beneath it draw
a jewel vajra of six parts and six fingers.
Having smeared the homa pit all over
with yellow earth and yellow perfume,
make the offerings, yellow flowers and the rest,
just as prescribed.
This is always to be done
in the northern part of a town and the like.
For magnetizing, on a red ground,
make the hearth in the shape of a womb,
marked with a lotus,
or else in the shape of a half-moon.
The pit, the rim and the rest
are made to the measure for pacifying.
Smear it with red earth, red perfume and the like,
and scatter red flowers and the like.
This is always to be done
in the western part of a town and the like.
For fierce rites, in the forest of the ancestors
and in the house of the mātṛkās,
it should be drawn on rough and cracked ground:
triangular, twenty fingers in measure,
dug ten fingers deep.
A three-pronged wrathful vajra,
like the light of a rain cloud,
about eight fingers in measure,
should be marked in its center.
Leaving three fingers from the pit,
surround it with a three-finger rim.
Smear it with black earth, ash from the charnel ground,
and whatever else is at hand,
and do it to the south of the maṇḍala house and so forth.
The remaining activities, too,
are included in the pits of the four activities,
yet once more I shall explain here
their particular pits; listen!
For summoning, make the homa pit
in the shape of a single-pronged vajra,
surrounded by vajra hooks;
the rest is the same as for magnetizing.
For binding it is square;
for stupefying it is like that for enriching,
in the direction of Īśāna, the northeast,
and the ground is made white and yellow.
For expelling it is like the fierce rite,
especially in the northwest, the quarter of the wind,
smeared with camel dung.
For making friends fall out
and hate one another,
one should likewise look to
the procedure of the fierce rites as prescribed.
Next the ritual that gathers
all activities shall be explained.
One who knows the reality of all activities
distinguishes them by intention and so forth.
On a ground that accords with the activity,
make a hearth that accords with the activity.
Wherever the mantra practitioner's mind delights,

it may also be set up in that spot.
To the measure of one cubit,
make it shaped like a lotus or an utpala.
Its rim is like a lotus;
the pit and the rim are slightly flared.
Inside, the basin and the rest are as for pacifying.
Mark the four directions with vajras.
Eight finger-widths in breadth
and one finger-width in height,
encircled by the weapons,
wheel, jewel, lotus and sword,
make the emblem of a vajra in the center.
With a vajra fence along the sides,
the hearth is round all over,
and below, a circle should be drawn.
This is the rite of the hearth.

The kindling sticks are of palāśa, udumbara and āmra, nyagrodha and palāśa, kadamba and other milky trees, made from the tip portion, twelve finger-widths in measure, fresh and with their leaves. Those whose heart or tip is damaged, those without bark, or split, short or very long, thin, thick or crooked, cracked, broken or worm-eaten, having two tips, infested with insects, or dried out: these are to be rejected for all virtuous rites, but are auspicious for sinful rites. Barley, sesame, milk and curd, śāli rice and priyaṅgu, blue kuśa grass and cūta leaves, flowers and white sandalwood, honey, butter and parched grain, dūrvā grass and fragrant liquids: these the yogin should burn for pacifying, as they are obtained. Of the woods for the sacrifice, the firewood should be lower than the hearth. Dry dung from the forest may also be used. Whatever is infested with insects should be rejected. This is the rite of the substances to be burned for pacifying: the kindling sticks, the firewood, the grains and so forth.

The kindling sticks are like those for pacifying,
but from the beautiful middle portion,
broken off to the length of one cubit.
Having sprinkled them with yellow-scented water,
smear them with curd, milk and butter,
and dip them in the three sweet substances.
Then excellent food with curd,
butter and black sesame,
śatapuṣpa together with butter,
honey and śāli rice,
other grains and lotus,
nāgakesara and bilva fruit,
together with dūrvā grass:
these should be burned for the rite of increase.
The firewood is as before, but in particular
it should be wood bearing fruit.

Campaka and aśoka,
punnāga and nāgakesara,
deep red and sour,
bearing flowers and fruit,
made from the branch portion,
five finger-widths in measure:
make these the kindling sticks for the rite of magnetizing.
The firewood too should be of trees
that are sour and bear flowers.

Foul-smelling flowers,
and likewise black grains
and chaff are to be scattered.
Likewise human bone powder,
poison and the like are to be offered.
Trees that are hot, bitter and thorny,
growing in a charnel ground,
made from the root portion,
the kindling sticks twelve finger-widths in measure,
sharp at the tip and rough,
two-tipped, smeared with poison and the like;
owl feathers and the like may also be used.
This is the firewood for the wrathful rite.

Next, the arrangement of the vessels for the offering water.
Made of gold or silver,
or of conch or mother-of-pearl,
of copper or bronze,
or of clay or leaves:
arrange them as one's means allow.
The argha vessel, with flowers and milk,
is placed in front.
Having fumigated it with fragrant incense,
recite one's own mantra over it seven times.
The water vessel is placed on the left.
This is the offering water for the rite of pacifying.
Water with curd and sesame
and with yellow scent and the like,
consecrated by reciting one's own mantra over it,
is the argha for increase.
For magnetizing, in a copper vessel or the like,
water with red scent and the like,
ablaze with red flowers,
filled to the brim, is to be made alluring.
Water that is black and foul-smelling,
made with blood,
or sour liquid and cow's urine,
or human excrement,
or donkey's urine may also be used.
Having recited the mantra interspersed with phaṭ
and placed it on the right side,
apply it to the wrathful rite.
This is the rite of the argha.

To calm illness and the like, pacifying
is done in the evening by one endowed with compassion.
The hero, facing east,
with ornaments such as flower garlands,
and clothing that is white,
seated on a soft cushion
in the lotus posture,
meditates on glorious Vairocana.
One-pointed and in silence,
visualizing the one to be accomplished
on a white water maṇḍala arisen from the syllable oṃ,
seated on a lion, lotus and moon seat,
by the sequence that will be set out below,
for the benefit of all sentient beings,
recite the mantra ending in svāhā
and always perform the rites of pacifying.

Rites of increase, for poverty, emaciation and the like,
are done joyfully in the morning.
Adorned with yellow garments and the like,
facing north,
seated in the auspicious posture,
or else in the posture of royal ease,
with white rice and curd
as food, the one who knows reality,
satisfied with food and drink,
meditates as Ratnasambhava.
On the maṇḍala of Mahendra, the earth,
arisen from the syllable laṃ,
visualizing the one to be accomplished
seated upon an elephant,
pronounce oṃ at the end of the mantra
and apply the rite of increase.

For magnetizing this is taught:
at night, with a passionate mind,
facing west,
clad in red ornaments,
seated in the sattva posture,
meditate supremely as Vajrarāga.
On the wind maṇḍala arisen from yaṃ,
in the soft lotus of the consort,
visualizing the one to be accomplished,
recite hoḥ at the end of the mantra.
Urging with red rays of light,
if the fire offering is done with sidelong glances,
women will be infatuated.

Against those who harm the Three Jewels,
who transgress the sacred commitments and are wrathful,
with a hostile mind, facing south,
at midnight or at midday,
adorned with blue clothing and the like,
on a hard and rough seat,
seated in the wrathful posture,
meditate as Vajrakrodha.
On a sun maṇḍala from raṃ,
visualizing the one to be accomplished,
the syllables hūṃ and phaṭ,
resounding like the sound of thunder,
are applied at the beginning, middle and end.
This is the rite of the actions concerning the one to be accomplished.

Then the glorious practitioner,
adorned with every ornament
and abiding in the samādhi of the two secrets,
should begin the rite of the fire offering.
The water vessel is placed on the left,
and the substances to be burned are placed on the right.
With the recitation of the mantra that accomplishes all rites,
one sprinkles all the substances.
Then, with auspicious words,
going round to the right, one spreads the kuśa grass.
It should be taken from the tips,
stainless and undamaged,
with its leaves, and blue in color,
neither too long nor too short.
Bent over, it is spread clockwise.
Then, in keeping with the rite,
according to the particular direction of the hearth,
and also pointing to the east, it is spread.
Having strewn it at the doorway with that,
then, inside the hearth,
the practitioner offers kuśa grass.
When the rite of the kuśa grass is complete,
one should begin the rite of the fire.
Fire produced from a fire drill,
or else, according to the particular rite,
fire from a twice-born and the like is also suitable.
Kindling it with a fan,
using firewood moistened with milk and so on,
one should kindle the fire so that it burns brightly.
When one knows that the fire is blazing,
one should then summon the fire god.
He is invited from the direction of fire,
surrounded by a retinue of sages,
arisen fully from the letter raṃ,
on a lotus arisen from oṃ,
seated upon a beautiful ram,
abiding in meditative concentration: so one should visualize him clearly.
His color, garments, ornaments and so on
should be made to accord with the rite.
His nature is the element of fire,
with a vast garland of flames.
He has one face and four arms;
vajra and trident, staff,
rosary, flask and ewer
are visualized in order in his right and left hands.
When the vajra fire has arrived,
he is requested to sit in the center of the hearth.
Then one offers arghya and the rest
and worships him according to the rite.
With the water that accomplishes all rites
one sprinkles, and then offers water for rinsing the mouth.
With the intention of requesting accomplishments,
one offers the ladle oblations into his mouth.
For virtuous rites, with the elbow
placed between the knees, one makes the oblation.
For fierce rites, the left hand
is held outside while one makes the oblation.

oṃ vajra anala mahābhūta jālaya sarvva bhasmiṃ kuru sarvva duṣṭāṃ vighāṃ hūṃ phaṭ

The fire mantra itself
is combined in accordance with the rite.

oṃ sarvva sampade svāhā
This is for curd and food.
oṃ agnaye svāhā
This is for the butter.
oṃ apratihate vajrāya svāhā
This is for the kuśa grass.
oṃ sarvva pāpaṃ dahana vajrāya svāhūṃ
With this, sesame is offered to burn away evil deeds.
oṃ vajra āyuṣe svāhā
This is for dūrvā grass, to increase lifespan.
oṃ vajra puṣṭaye svāhā
This is for rice, to make the household prosper.

Other grains and so on should also be smeared with butter here and burned.

After that, sprinkling, mouth-rinsing water and so on;
the fire, being satisfied,
dissolves completely into the blaze.
Then, in the center of the fire, one's own deity,
with ornaments, garments, color and the rest
in accordance with the rite,
is visualized, and one examines the signs of the fire.
By hues such as gold
the color of the fire is to be known.
For pacifying, a white color is auspicious;
for increasing, one like gold;
for subjugating, the color of broken coral;
for fierce rites, black
and blue and so on are to be known as the signs.
As to shape, the śrīvatsa,
the vajra, the wheel and so forth,
adorned with its own emblem, is auspicious.
If the fire is very fragrant,
it grants the accomplishment of virtuous rites.
The smell of a corpse, the smell of fish,
heavy smoke and crackling noise,
sparks that burn the mantra practitioner:
these are bad signs of the fire.
The signs of nonvirtue
are auspicious for nonvirtuous rites;
those very same, for auspicious rites,
are not auspicious signs.
Then, having driven out the obstructors,
one begins the fire offering once again.
Then, with one's own mantra,
having sprinkled the fire,
one fills the filling ladle according to the rite
and offers the ladle oblation three times.

oṃ agnaye amukasya śāntiṃ kuru svāhā

This is done with that mantra.

In order to satisfy the deities, one first recites the three-syllable mantra, and, combining it with one's own mantra, one makes the burnt offerings in their proper order. The order of burning is this: first, the kindling sticks; after that, butter; then wheat, sesame and the like; then foods and the like; then porridge. Then, having burned the assembled offerings of curd, milk, fruits and the like, things to be licked, drinks and so on, the yogin offers flowers, tooth-cleansers and the like. In the same way, having worshiped one's own deity as well, one should request the accomplishment of one's desired aims. Then, having requested the lord to depart, the disciple once more worships the fire god and requests him to depart; he will then grant the yogin the desired result. This is the rite of the fire offering.

Of gold, or silver, or copper,
or else of sacrificial wood,
the filling ladle, its bowl, its handle and the rest:
their measure, shape and so on are explained.
At the top it has four finger-widths;
its length is made square,
and it is hollowed two finger-widths deep.
In its center it is adorned with a wisdom vajra.
Above that, a spout of four finger-widths
is made like a lotus petal.
In its middle, a hole through which the essence drips
is made the size of the little finger.
Then below comes the handle,
adorned with a vajra jewel of six finger-widths.
The filling ladle is the mother who grants the Dharma;
the pouring ladle is the means, endowed with savor,
which benefits all beings.
It is two finger-widths wide and one finger-width deep,
its mouth like a lotus petal,
beautified in the middle by a vajra;
the rest is to be shown as before.
With one holding five finger-widths
the full oblation is said to be made;
with one holding three finger-widths
the other fire offering is said to be made.
For pacifying, it is held with the mudrā of supreme awakening;
for increasing, with the jewel fist;
for subjugating and summoning,
it is likewise held with the lotus fist.
For fierce rites, with effort,
one likewise makes the wrathful fist.
This is the rite of the filling ladle and the pouring ladle.

The ladle oblations refers to the full oblation and the rest.

I shall explain its further procedures.
For pacifying, increasing and subjugating,
which are virtuous rites,
the face should be smiling.
For fierce rites, with a wrathful face
one burns the kindling sticks and the rest.
What was not explained in the Tattvasaṃgraha, the Cakrasaṃvara,
the guhyaka, the Vajrabhairava,
the Tilaka and others:
this is the fire offering condensed in summary form.

United with all the ḍākas,
Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata:
thus spoke the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

This is the forty-eighth chapter of the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, which sets out the sequence of the fire offering rite, the great secret of all activities.

Once again the goddess danced,
and she addressed these words:
"What are the small ones and the rest?
How are they to be distinguished?
Of the varied natures of the constitutions,
and the characteristics of the substances that were taught,
doubt has arisen in me.
Great Sage, please explain."
The Blessed One said:
"Now I shall explain the distinctions
of the small ones and the rest.
I shall explain the characteristics of substances endowed with qualities;
listen, goddess Prajñā."

Those to whom the small and the rest belong are the small ones and the rest. The word "the rest" refers to the middling, the great, and the greatest of the great. These divisions of condition are fourfold, and one should distinguish them as four. Women and men are to be known in their particulars through the distinction of their natures, such as wind, bile and phlegm, and the substances are to be applied accordingly. To one of wind constitution one should give oily substances; to one of bile constitution, cooling substances are beneficial when given; and to one of phlegm constitution one gives bitter and astringent substances. Again, rough, cool and oily substances are recommended respectively for those of wind, bile and phlegm constitution.

The words "Men and women too are each held to be of three kinds" mean that they are held to be of three definite kinds by the nature of their constitutions. The word "too" indicates examination, because the elixir is not given to a eunuch.

"Black" is the color black; it is recommended for flowing. "Tawny" is also a color; it is for emission. "Pale blue" is the combination of black and yellow; it is for both flowing and emission. "And" means that they are applied in their respective order. "Rough" means rough like a cow's tongue. "Soft" is the one called padminī. "Firm" means that the fluid is hard: one whose fluid has become thick. "With bumps" is the śaṅkhinī, who has fleshy bumps on the lotus. These women have power over the binding of the essence.

"Stout" means that the face, the mouth and the breasts are full, the mouth large and stout. "Short" means short in stature and short-haired, smelling of liquor and intoxicated with desire; these are said to be of moderate stature. "Long" means that the face, the hands and the feet are long in length. Moreover, a fluid smelling of fish flows for a long time: this is the śaṅkhinī. "Like the pale blue one" means an unpleasant voice, with short limbs and short hair. "Rough-limbed" means an unpleasant smell and a slow gait: this is the citriṇī. "Long-necked": she whose neck is high is long-necked. She has a radiance like a white lotus, is lovely, and always has the sweet fragrance of pollen. Her eyes are like a deer's, she gazes like a deer, her neck and belly are like a deer's, and her waist is like that of the king of beasts: she is to be known as a padminī.

"Eyes red like copper" means that the eyes, the feet and the hands are very red, with the fragrance of jasmine; she delights in the family of the Sugata, and likewise grants the accomplishment of bliss. As for pi no unta, pi na is that which holds milk, the breast. It is firm, the hair is curly, the face is round all over, and there are three lines at the center of the navel: she is to be known as the garlanded one. "The common woman" is one who gives wealth. "Breastless" means of bad character and lacking breasts like a man, and so on up to "with incomplete limbs." "Faded in color" means a rough complexion. "Scent of śāli" means having a pleasing scent like śāli rice. "Liquor" is strength: being endowed with an intention perfect in samaya and conduct. "Scent other than liquor" means especially following the smell of corpses. "Intoxicated" means exceedingly distinguished.

"Two parts" means two parts, combining two by two.
