"Three parts" means those two parts transformed into three: hairless, fleshy, raised like the back of a tortoise, firm like an elephant's frontal boss, with a wide womb, and with blood and fluid always flowing. These six are the fortunate women, su bha ga, and they grant the supreme to the practitioner. These are the distinctions of the hips. By this same one, flowing like honey, with the scent of honey: she has the nature of honey.

"Separately" means that it is applied separately, distinctly, for the purpose of samaya and for others. "Otherwise," that is, other than for the welfare of beings, for rasāyana and the like, it is never to be done; otherwise not even a word of it is to be applied. Here it is a separate practice. In the phrase "by the device of mental intention," mental intention is what arises from the mind, and the device is a mechanical device made of wood and leather. "Accomplish" means that one should accomplish by means of it. "One girl" is the first: for the sake of awakening and liberation. "Two" are for the sake of samaya. "Many" are for the sake of wealth. "To be examined" refers especially to the eighteen substances. "Made clear" is because a single one grants infinite qualities. To whom? To the foremost of practitioners. "Producing qualities" means that by the nectar of the bird-faced channel itself, one becomes habituated to that quality again and again.

"Child" means one whose vigor has not yet opened: a young girl. "Twenty" means up to twenty years of age. "They should be purified by method" means that they themselves, being the field in which the seed is sown, are to be purified; and they themselves are the method, since they bring near and bestow the desired aim and make the fruit manifest, and so on. "Subtle" means lovely: a domain like space. In the phrase "the place of seizing by the powerful one," "powerful" means having capacity; "seizing" means making one's own; "place" means form, that is, postures such as the do la and the kurbba, which bring down and propel upward. In the phrase "the substance purifies," the substance is the ocean of existence.

Because it purifies and benefits, carrying one out of that ocean like a boat, it is "thoroughly purifying."

"All-covering" means the following. The mantrin should first embrace her all over, like a mongoose; kiss like a dove; take her lower lip between the teeth and suck it; seize her by the hair; apply the nails; strike with the fist; bite and tear with the teeth; and scratch with the nails at the armpits, the breasts, the waist, both thighs, the throat and the back, all accompanied by the sound "sīt." In addition, these acts that win over her mind, such as squeezing the breasts, are to be combined with the eight ways of performing each of them.

As for applying the nails: on one who is soft and young, they should be applied and drawn only to the measure of a kuśa blade. On the waist, the back, above the bhaga, at the hair, the cheeks and so on, marks the size of a grain of rice, of whatever kind is suitable, are to be made on those whose passion is slack. Nor is it the nails alone: she is to be satisfied with kissing and the rest.

For those whose passion is firm, one shapes three long fingers like a snake's head, inserts them into the opening of the vagina, and rubs until the mantra "sīt" comes forth. Satisfying older women and the like in this way is called the vajra finger.

For those who are young and tender, one inserts the tongue at the channel where the awakening mind, bearing its own sign and radiance, flows down, and gives joy merely by curling it. This is called the vajra tongue-spoon.

When the practitioner stirs her in this way again and again, her heart trembles, she grows intoxicated with joy, and the water of the subtle particles of the mind trickles forth. Free of shame and the like, her hair standing on end, she gazes with intense passion, grows eager to embrace, and says "sīt."

Then, assuming the cross-legged posture and uniting vajra and lotus evenly, one should display the postures (karaṇas) set out in all the treatises, such as dola, kurbbaṇa, suci, utkṣepa, suprasari, grāmanāgara, preṅkhena and uruspoda. All of these are declared to be the mudrā of Vajrasattva. Because through them one fully accomplishes the accomplishment of one's own aim, the statement that the substance arising from all these joys is what thoroughly purifies is itself called "profound."

Further, as long as the man practices in an inferior way the rasāyana of the sense objects, then even after entering the wheel of joy, practicing as an inferior man is what is called "not emitting."

"By the path of all activities" refers to the path of awakening, which is called profound. It consists in practicing by gathering into one all the faculties, outer and inner, such as the eye, and all the objects, such as form, and by gathering together all the enjoyments, each abiding in its own mode and its own share. Otherwise, taken separately, one would not come to enjoy them all. Because that very enjoyment leads one onward, it is the path.

"The great tree of mercury" means this: just as a wish-fulfilling tree grants every desired aim, so too this awakening mind makes every result its domain.

As for the lines:
"Hence the water-born, endowed with qualities,
Is the confidence that destroys its own faults,"
that which shows and clarifies the meaning is confidence; it is what views suchness.

This extends as far as being uplifted by the empowerment of the wisdom of the mudrā. The wisdom of the mudrā is of two kinds: through the various accomplishments and through the entry of bliss, it is to be understood as knowing and what is to be known.

Thus the confidence arising from all the faculties brings perfect glory, youth, and the bliss of freedom from illness. By enjoying a partner endowed with glory, youth and passion, one's mastery, health and strength increase, one lives long for the sake of sentient beings, and through that very supreme bliss one views suchness; and through confidence it becomes unerring accomplishment. The passage extends up to this point.

"One's own" means one's own semen. Through it come pacifying, enriching, subjugating, summoning and every other rite. It is this that is taught as the chief rasāyana, called "elixir."

As for "the act of the substance, the womb-born not old": "not old" means not aged, free of worms and the like, and endowed with the proper characteristics. "Womb-born" is a firstborn issued from the womb who has died. "Act" is its function: invisibility and the like, as well as transforming into various forms; and all the operations of elixirs and so on should be applied.

As for "the producer's aim destroys aging": by whatever power of its own, the producer's aim destroys aging; "producer" means the one who brings forth. That substance, the producer's aim itself, destroys aging. Destroying aging and the rest also refers, for instance, to curing ocaka diseases of the faculties with the corpse of a child that has remained eight months in the womb.

As for "mica, supreme for binding mercury": "supreme" means it extends to what is clear and made directly evident.

As for "elixir is the portion endowed with miraculous power": miraculous power is flying through the sky and the like. "Portion" means part, that is, the mass of faults; it is accomplished through being free of these.

"The four mercuries" are pārada, the near-essence, the essence-bearer, and the lord of elixirs. "The four micas" are distinguished as white, yellow, blue and red. Each of these is in turn of four kinds, distinguished as peacock-neck, snake, frog and cloud. The peacock-neck kind is bluish-red, like the feathers of a peacock's neck. The blue is the great essence and is to be taken especially. The snake form, as it increases in particular, takes on the shape of a snake. The frog form is like the gait of a frog. The cloud form is like the movement of a rain cloud.

As for the lines: "If the strong one and the rest lack essence, Various faults arise as before," the strong one is sulphur. Its faults are heaviness, oiliness and hardness, and through these faults it becomes as before. Blackness, instability and heaviness are the faults of mica and of elixir. Stoniness, dampness, heaviness, looking as if eaten by insects, and bluntness are the faults of the sharp one, iron; through these five faults it is as before. Containing stones is also a fault of sulphur. That of orpiment is a pale yellowness. That of pearl is likewise pale yellowness, lacking a core. That of coral is a lack of luster. That of bell metal is gold-stone.

That of sapphire is a lack of golden flecks. That of jewels is being pierced. That of gold is adulteration with counterfeit metal. That of silver is cracking, hardness and the like. That of lead is harm to the limbs. That of tin is being long like a snake. That of bell metal, turquoise, vimala and the like is a grey pallor. With all these defects, too, it is as before: this is the procedure for precious substances.

"Without exception" means that it applies also to other things, such as animal products and roots. That of the svasti leaf, too, is a yellowish pallor. That of the badasi pod, too, is being counterfeit and yellowish-pale. That of the nail perfume (nakha) is being like a pig's ear. The great defect of the deer product is a smell like fish, alkali and the like, and turning quickly to ash when burned; it should be rejected. That of the sea "scorpion," too, is being somewhat yellow and pale; it should be rejected for all rites. That of turmeric is lack of color, pallor and thinness. That of calamus the practitioner should recognize by its being green. That of costus is being like sea foam when broken. That of kakkola is being uneven like the shape of a frog, and light like chaff. The defect of nutmeg (jātīphala) is pallor, lack of oil and a faint sound. The defect of cardamom and the three fruits is being light like chaff; they give rise to the three faults. That of reṇuka is floating on water like black pepper. That of priyaṅgu is being green, which is contrary. That of saffron the wise recognize as thinness, being like khadira, small stamens, a slight yellowness, and lack of color like dark butter. The defect of uśīra is a greenish sheen and shortness; it should always be rejected. That of kaivarti nut-grass is growing from turbid water and growing among algae; it should be rejected. That of nut-grass is growing from damp ground. That of citraka is a green color. That of sehali is lacking a root. That of kusuma is thinness. That of campaka should be recognized by its opening wide. That of ketakī is having many offshoots.

Listen, great goddess of great fortune,
to the places where essences and substances arise!

They arise from the peaks of the snowy Himālaya, from Mount Mahendra and from the crags of the Vindhya, and also from crags in dense forest from which streams flow like collyrium. I shall also explain the places where essences are empowered.

Where swarms of bees adorn the face of the sky,
where gusts of wind do not stir, yet wind-born snow falls,
pervaded by the scent of many trees laden with fruit,
where hosts of gods in the sky make the sound of the buddhas resound,
delightful, playing with the fragrance of many sweet scents,
the ground adorned with the fallen fruits of many trees:
such mountain slopes with flowing water are said to be the places where essences arise.

Among these, from white crags arise jewels such as crystal and water crystal. From yellow crags where yellow water flows arise coral, red ochre, and jewels like the bow of Śakra (Indra), that is, the rainbow; and the jīva jewel arises from red mountains. From mountain regions that are blue-green and warm, with vapor like smoke and with dark earth and stones, arise gold ore, bell metal and other things, and the four kinds of lodestone. From blue-green crags arise many things, such as vimala and the like, sapphire, and jewels such as stamba. From crags of mixed blue and red colors arise realgar, orpiment, sulphur and the like. From damp crags with living creatures arise alkaline salts such as borax. To gather them, one should first offer a torma, and then gather them in a gourd vessel. Mercury that is produced from stone by the procedure using fresh urine is supreme.

These sciences, great goddess,
are the highest of all preparations.
If Brahmā, Śakra and others succeed by them,
what need is there to speak of others?
In times gone by, long ago,
in the first eon of great bliss,
there dwelt the great Vajrasattva,
the one supreme god.
Seeing the asuras, the siddhas and others,
the hosts of gods grew frightened;
all the buddhas assembled
and swiftly urged me on.
What melted into drops, great goddess,
and was scattered all about by the māras,
that which bewilders all,
is known by the name of mercury.
That which comes from the depths of the water is inferior;
that which is born from the mountains is supreme.
Now, the preparations of fragrances
I shall explain again; listen, goddess.
White and red,
and especially yellow:
I shall explain their divisions.
They are best, inferior and middling.
Sweet-smelling like ketaka,
or else oily, is the best musk.
The khadasi pod grown in dry country,
large and round, is auspicious.
The "scorpion" blue and oily,
and arising from the ocean, is good.
Nail perfume like a horse's hoof,
like an elephant's ear,
and like the petals of badarī and utpala:
these are four kinds.
Blend them two by two with wood for incense.
Aloeswood (aguru) heavy,
oily and like a crow's beak is auspicious.
Chawa shaped like a finger
and free of impurity is highly praised.
Yellow mura is best;
know the pale ones to be inferior.
Calamus thick, yellow outside
and white within, is good.
Naṅga like the antler of a mighty stag
is beautiful and has fine qualities.
Turmeric yellow like kuṭaja,
thick, with large joints,
and the color of collyrium when broken,
is wholesome for the work of essences.
That which is slightly blue in color,
and smells of hara when broken,
that turmeric is like dark butter
and is said to be inferior for the work of essences.
Rana like mung beans,
blue, is said to be the best.
Priyaṅgu grown in the forest,
like sahadeva leaves, is best.
Saffron like red safflower,
oily, with thin stamens, is best;
with thick, yellow stamens
and a scent of honey it is middling.
Nutmeg with a loud sound,
heavy and oily, is most precious.
Kakkola even,
heavy and soundless, is good.
Uśīra that is white,
long and intertwined is likewise praised.
Kaivarti nut-grass
born on riverbanks and lakeshores
and in water free of impurity,
free of algae, leaves and the like,
very fragrant and lovely, is supreme.
Chatiba cooked to the color
of bezoar is praised.
Nut-grass grown in dry country and hard is good;
grown in a region of even moisture it is middling.
Myrobalan grows in the forests
of the Vindhya and on Mount Mahendra;
that grown by the waters of the Narmadā is supreme,
the others inferior and middling.
The Fasting One, Abhayā,
Jīvantī, and likewise Amṛtā,

Pūtanā, and rohiṇī,
And vijayā: these are said to be the seven.
The one with three lines is cetakī;
Abhayā has five lines.
Jīvantī should be known as golden in color.
Amṛtā is said to be the very fleshy one,
And likewise pūtanā is the fleshless one.
The tawny one is called rohiṇī;
The one shaped like a gourd is vijayā.
For fragrance one should apply cetakī;
For rejuvenation, amṛtā is praised.
Abhayā and vijayā are middling;
The rest are of no use.

Emblic myrobalan is very fleshy,
Its stone is small, and it is full of flavor.
Grown on a rocky mountain
And fully ripe, it is declared the best.
Belleric myrobalan may be
Grown on an ordinary mountain, or elsewhere as well.
The harītakī that is oily and dark,
Heavy and buttery, is good.

Now listen as I explain
How each of these is to be gathered.
The substances should be gathered when grown in their proper season
And still possessing their potency.
Gathered in accord with this, they become beneficial;
Gathered contrary to it, they become harmful.

Now, Goddess, listen as I explain
The rite of satisfying.
Madhī and peṣṭhī,
And likewise godhi and jagala:
These are the grain liquors and prepared liquors,
With their various kinds of flavor;
They are stated in that order.
Madhī is explained as fivefold;
Peṣṭhī is said to be eightfold;
Godi is sevenfold;
And likewise jagala is threefold.

Now listen as I fully explain
The methods of brewing and compounding.
The first is made from honey;
The second is that of dates;
The third is made from grapes;
The fourth is made from a gathering of essences;
The fifth is called Mahendra,
And is declared the chief of them all.
Wide Open, and Tawny,
Intoxicating, and Source of All,
Fair Color made from lodhra,
Supreme Gold, jibo,
And Peaceful: these are the eight kinds.
These eight, made from peṣṭhī,
Are stated here in brief.
The first is made from sugarcane;
The second is that of sīdhu;
The third is made from two kinds of molasses;
The fourth is called Gray;
Various, and likewise Another,
And the one called Fearless is the seventh.
Fruit juice,
Flowers, and likewise others.

Taking the fruit of nyaducan and cakrāṅga,
Sixty-four of black pepper,
Ten srang of emblic juice,
With half a srang of posoncha,
Knead them in one measure of water
And brew it in the light of the sun.

Sahara datri flowers,
Coriander, dill,
Malaya and vetiver,
The mountain-born maiden,
Nutmeg, cinnamon, and posoncha:
Two zho of each of these,
With eight srang of sugarcane juice
And four times as much water.
Nīla, kaṇṭakārī,
Indian incense, small cardamom, tamāla,
Madder and nāgakesara,
Vetiver and black pepper,
Elephant long pepper,
Together with one srang of molasses,
Are poured into seven srang of water.
Indian incense, bitumen, tamāla,
Indian incense and costus, one part of each:
Into one measure of grain liquor
Put them all together, and for seven days
Brew it in the light of the sun.
Then add an equal amount of honey
With eight parts of jambu wood,
And once again, as before,
Brew it in the sun.
When only the measure of the honey remains,
Fumigate it with incense.
Then for one month
Place it in a rice granary.
Then beneath the ground
Leave it for a month and four days.
At first and at last pour equal amounts;
In between, pour fourfold.
Fumigate it with duhika, camphor and musk.
This is called Coquette.

Half a srang of datri flowers,
Molasses equal to the liquor,
And when the remaining portion is complete,
Afterwards mix them together.
A sixteenth part of the liquor,
As many datri flowers,
And a fragrant mash:
Stir all of these together.
Add to it month by month,
Pouring Coquette into it.
At first pour in an equal amount;
In the second month make it double;
And so on, three, four, five, six, seven,
Adding to it until a year has passed.
Then for six months
Seal it and keep it hidden.
It should be added to with molasses juice;
This is said to nourish the Greatly Intoxicating.

Eight ripe plantain fruits,
Likewise eight srang of Coquette,
And one srang of phurmo powder
Are poured into a bamboo
And left in grain for half a month.
This is called Supreme Passionate.

The three fruits, shashi,
Aloeswood and arjuna,
Substances together with jasmine flowers:
Take one srang of their powder,
Again with eight srang of Coquette,
And leave it for seven days.
This is called Flower Intoxicant.

Grapes, katam, shingwangchuk,
Date juice,
Two kinds of cane root,
With pata flowers and the like:
These eight kinds should be compounded.
This is the method of preparing the ferment.

What is made from peṣṭhī is the Intoxicant;
What is made from flour is Supreme Watery.
What is made from the husks of mango and the like
Is called liquor.
Jagala is called grain beer.
Grapes and honey make the Sole Hero.
Sour, Gyönchen, and Zanmo;
What is made from tamarind root
Is known as Nyachichen.
Infused with flowers, it is the Greatly Intoxicating;
With fragrance, it is explained as Coquette.
For those who refuse food, the emaciated,
And the bilious, jagala is beneficial.
For the phlegmatic, flour liquor is beneficial.
For both, jibo is praised.
Through compounding it is rejuvenating, supreme among flavors.
Cakrāṅga and honey,
Costus and the juice of nutgrass,
Mango, kumpa, and others as well
Should be compounded into the ferment for liquor.
This is the rite of liquor and ferment.

Cyperus and kuca,
turmeric and white garlic:
purify these for three days in sour rice-water.
Then boil them
in a decoction of the five leaves.
With rice flour and molasses,
washed with śigru juice and goat's urine,
they become pure.
If musk is purified
in śobhañja and goat's urine,
it will not lose its fragrance.
As for the scent gland of the khadasi,
first rub off its hair with ash.
Then cook it
in a decoction of the five leaves,
and purify it again
in śigru juice and goat's urine.
Put it inside a ketakī
and set it at the root of a sopañca.
That is the purification of the khadasi gland.
Sweet flag and the two turmerics,
boiled in equal parts of mustard oil,
vajra milk and sour rice-water,
become pure; of this there is no doubt.
With castor juice
together with water of bitumen,
wash the sehalle,
wrap it in leaves and smear it with dung.
Hidden beneath the earth, it is purified.
Kusuma and the fruits
are first purified in buttermilk,
then washed, placed
inside a śrīphala and heated; thus they are purified.
Sulphur becomes pure through ghee;
orpiment is purified with beef;
vyāghrī is purified with cow dung;
realgar is purified with its own sour liquid;
saffron is purified with butter;
cawa with bull's urine;
agaru is purified with blood;
sihla is purified with a girl's blood.

Then the goddess made offerings to the Blessed One, bowed to him, and said:

"Where should one begin
the supreme application of rasāyana?
How are the connections
of the categories explained before?
What is the purpose
of the sequence of applications you have explained?
The rite of cooking black butter,
and the samādhi connected with the object:
how are the eighteen elements that were taught
gathered with one another?
The kinds of device you have explained:
how are they outer and inner?
How do the various diseases that arise
ripen and become results?
How many kinds of the coarse are there?
What is the distinction of the subtle?
I do not understand the substances and rasāyana.
The applications you have explained:
what are their distinctions of time?
About these, doubt has arisen in me.
Great Bliss, please explain!"

He declared:

"You who sport with every illusion,
listen, goddess Mahāmāyā!
The essence gathered like honey
is secret in all the tantras.
Having worshiped the place and the rest as is proper,
one should perform the approach of the Cakrasaṃvara mantra.
Having richly worshiped the twenty-four
heroes and yoginīs, who are nondual,
that great yogin
should then begin the application of rasāyana medicine.
The vidyādhara's companion
is devoted to the approach of mantra,
without weariness and without doubts,
devoted to the conduct of samaya,
brave, steady and without guile,
gentle and diligent in serving the guru.
Together with such a one should one accomplish it.
If the practitioner acts otherwise,
the fruit of siddhi will not arise.
Having purified the vidyā explained before,
first one should give the samaya.
One who holds the naked observance
confers with the second the empowerments of name and the rest.
Having empowered, one teaches suchness.
The taste free of empty and not empty and the rest
is to be realized by the supreme person.
The substance that comes from the nine doors
is called the coarse.
Substance is that which brings about digestion.
Essence is the residue of silver.
Taking is explained as sattva;
sattva is called substance,
and silver is called the melting.
Thus substance and melting
combined are called taking.
Darkness is called the subtle;
the subtle has the characteristic of the space element.
It cooks the distinctions of the aggregates and the rest
and all the elements.
The bringing forth of various forms
is impelled by my wisdom.
These, greatly fortunate goddess,
are the combinations of substances and elements.
Those of feeble diligence, the base,
and those of little merit will not see them.
Since diligence is the ground of every elixir,
therefore one should take up diligence.
The elixir, its binding and its healing,
I have explained in brief.
Through the supreme full ripening,
the hundred faults, binding and the rest,
that attend the peerless elixir
the intelligent should examine and avert.
The elixir is black and blue;
the elixir is perfectly accomplished.
By the rite of mercury and lhangtser,
until the great elixir is accomplished,
for that long strive and examine.
The device that has a wide mouth
is the karana and the vulture's mouth.
The device that is like a clay drum
is the donkey's mouth and the camel's mouth.
The vulture's mouth
should be fitted with a reed tube.
The karanada device
should be fitted with a sword sheath.
The device called the donkey's mouth
is joined to a tube like a heron's neck.
The device of the camel's mouth
is made to resemble a crow's beak.
What is to be subdued is the frog:
with the reed device it is cooked into honey;
with the crow-beaked one it is cooked into a bee,
a stone slab blazing and radiant;
with the heron-beaked one it is cooked into an elephant;
with the device of the sword sheath
it is cooked into a lion.
The great elixir, dismayed and enraged,
scattering and thoroughly cooked,
will flee into the water.
Then, having given it the elephant-head elixir,
sulphur and the rest,
it is subdued and pressed down by burning,
and having been cast down onto the ground,
it trembles, swoons, is wrapped in smoke,
and gives out a bubbling sound.
Then aśvagandha,
bala, cakramarda,
bala, taka and śigru,
paricchada, kumuta,
utpala, mālikā seed,
juniper, bone and kī seed,
gourd seed and madu,
posön and the bark of palāśa:
make these into powder and paste,
and then pound it in that.
With fixed proportions it is bound;
the substances should be given in a sixth part.
Then knead it in a stone mortar.
First, for a while, it should be purified.
Then with rock salt,
together with pig's fat and blood,
by stirring, its faults are to be destroyed.
I shall explain the placing of the device.
Together with sandalwood, ash medicine and the rest,
place it inside a bone.
First do this one hundred and eight times.
Afterwards, the elixir together with the belly-born
is put inside a reed
and set in the place explained before."

"Root and fruit" means the root and fruit of sopañja and the like. For katumaja, clay and the like, their flowers, leaves, bones and so on are to be taken. This is the rite of the application for cooking the elixir.

Thistle in parts as many as the directions,
and mercury in parts as many as the senses:
by fixed proportion it becomes bound,
and one should add to it step by step in tenths.
Give fragrant substances each month.
With gold the complexion becomes fine;
with silver the teeth become fine;
pearl makes the eyes clear;
sapphire benefits the hair of the head and body.
Diamond turns back lightning;
iron turns back water and weapons.
With thistle, Viśveśvara and the mind,
Śakra (Indra), Agni, Yama, Varuṇa,
Kubera and the rest, and all obstructors, are turned back.
With nāgapar and fresh butter,
together with sea salt,
this supreme preparation of mercury
destroys old age and leprosy.
The medicine called ox-tongue,
mixed with mercury and applied as an ointment,
clears away hemorrhoids.
It is nourished with karikesa;
with girikarṇikā
and red spikenard it is melted and killed.
With dzak, aśvagandhā,
nāgabalā and balikā
in equal parts, one should make it swoon.
With the juice of datura
it exercises all its own qualities.

Next I shall explain the rite of oil
that gathers every essence.
Oil is said to be of two kinds:
arising from the inanimate world and from living beings.
First, that of the inanimate world is to be explained.
With the water of the three great fruits
moisten the oil-yielding seeds again and again.
When they have been dried well,
the mantrin grows them in a plot of ground
free from faults such as dust.
They should be well ripened and oily,
black, and free from faults such as paleness.
Pound them well and warm them,
then cleanse them with emblic juice and rainwater.
Infuse those firm seeds with fumigation,
fumigating them just enough over a smokeless fire.
Then press them in the presses.
In the three seasons of spring, summer and autumn,
cook the medicinal oil in the light of the sun.
In early winter, late winter and the rainy season, these three,
cook the oil just enough over a fire.
Gandhapatra, cinnamon bark,
cardamom and nāgakesara,
spikenard and salila:
make these into powder
and pour it into oil that comes from sesame,
making the water four times the oil.
Add half a sang of aloeswood,
making it four times the medicine,
and then boil it over a moderate fire.
Again, spikenard and nutgrass,
sandalwood, cinnamon and leaf,
in powder equal to that described before;
cook it likewise, as before.
Again, storax, dark devadāru,
costus, jala and bathar:
mix them all, put in one sang
together with eight sang of oil,
pour it into a bamboo vessel,
and cook it in the sun for seven days.
It gives beauty, good fortune and great strength,
and desired aims are perfectly accomplished.
If brahma oil is added,
it bestows the supreme on the yogin.
Further, bala and cardamom,
pata and bhṛṅgarāja,
nakha, costus and viśaca,
respectively in four parts, six, seven,
and twelve parts:
these, as a paste of medicine,
in the proportions described before,
cook in the bamboo vessel as described before.
When marrow oil is added,
it carries off all diseases.
Then mix it together
with oil of the great flesh,
pour it into a vessel of honey and warm it in the sun.
Within seven days
it becomes like musk.
This preparation is excellent,
unsurpassed among all protections.
The fruit of titraca,
with sandalwood, storax and cinnamon,
and the oil described before
mixed with marrow fat,
placed in a vessel of honey as before, and so on:
it becomes like camphor
and bewilders all people.
Then, with mountain-born oil,
the three fruits, nutgrass, shugé,
pūti, white ta, kusuma,
the vigor of sepala,
and saffron as well,
pour them into a vessel of honey
and cook them seven days in the sun's warmth.
It brings good fortune, increases strength, and gives radiance.
One should always drink milk
and strive in meditative concentration and recitation.
Always abiding in celibacy,
one should hold back the semen.
Together with honey,
the practitioner should eat the fine substance for six months.
Collect all of his excrement
and grind it into powder together with the fine substance.
Place it in a new pot.
Make the powder of the three hot spices and cora
double in amount,
and mix in a tenth part of purgative powder.
Make pills with molasses.
If the practitioner, deeply composed,
eats two zho each day,
all diseases
are destroyed, and hemorrhoids especially are overcome.
Mix four parts of the Grandfather
with sixteen of garlic,
and drink it together with milk.
Within seven days
colic and diseases of the brain are destroyed.
If one drinks it as before
together with thin buttermilk,
it clears away all diseases.
By practicing the preparation for six months
one is rid of white hair and wrinkles.
Flour of red rice,
the root and leaves of balā,
and two zho of garlic together:
grind them and make pills,
and cook them in butter or sesame oil.
Having eaten them, drink urine in suitable measure.
By this, broken bones
are joined, and strength and vigor increase.
Bhṛṅgarāja and brahma herb,
fresh, should be eaten with urine;
this removes poison.
Two zho of turmeric juice
with an equal amount of urine,
if drunk, overcomes the poison of moving creatures,
swellings and all diseases.
Turmeric juice, cow's urine,
and likewise the chief of the Bhūtas,
in sang equal to the number of the Vedas:
of this drink two zho at a time,
increasing it a little in the spring season.
This chief of preparations
clears away poisons and fevers.
Nutgrass, emblic,
the sharp-scented one and great fat,
with honey, in equal parts, as a powder,
place it in a vessel of magnetic iron.
By this sequence of preparation
make up to three hundred sang.
It is called Vasanta,
and it grants all the siddhis.
By applying it for one year
one becomes attractive and the mind becomes sharp.
One is freed from all diseases,
and wrinkles and white hair are cleared away.
Though exposed to the seasonal winds, the heat of the sun and the like,
one need have no fear.

Those who rely on other applications
Will gain no fruit and will go astray.
Always practicing meditative concentration and recitation,
One lives for up to three hundred years.

Long pepper and saffron,
Prepared in equal parts,
Should be eaten in the summer season.
If one then drinks warm urine,
Food becomes appetizing and is digested.

Juice of nirguṇḍī,
Mixed with urine, is given as a nasal medicine;
It removes diseases arising from phlegm.
That same, mixed with the impurity of the lotus,
Applied as an ointment, removes elephantiasis.

Mix the root of marakada
With powder of teeth and nails.
First wash the wound with butter;
If one then anoints it with that medicine,
It is supreme for healing wounds.

Brick powder and cardamom powder,
Kneaded in the juice of girikarṇa,
If smeared on the eyes,
Are supreme for destroying dimness of sight.

Cleanse with the juice of the three fruits.
Leaves of mahāhataka,
Milk, and ligbumik,
Together with the water of the Bhūta,
Are poured into the eyes; then, if fumigated
With lodhra, the eyes become free of impurity.

Powder of the two turmerics in equal parts,
With two measures of cow's buttermilk,
Is boiled until one measure remains.
If one drinks it for seven days,
The body flourishes.
This application of rasāyana
Was taught by the Hero himself.

Turmeric and brick,
Likewise red ochre and juice of datura:
Staying in a windless place,
If one rubs them on the body,
They always increase strength
And are of benefit for binding mercury.

Costus, murba, śuktika,
And cinnamon, powdered in equal parts,
With a double portion of mangsitsa:
If one rubs these on, one's wishes are fulfilled.

Shilajit and brick,
Nirguṇḍī and cardamom,
Prepared in equal parts,
With a fourfold portion of turmeric,
And half a part of the juice of the golden tree:
Rubbed on, this removes poison;
And rubbed on together with urine,
It destroys scabies and leprosy.

The flesh of a boy and of a young maiden,
With butter, as is suitable,
If eaten continually, gives long life.
The flower that comes from the Beautiful One,
If eaten, gradually destroys old age.
Camphor and sandalwood, always
Relied upon, are supreme for dispelling diseases.
Likewise the four in equal parts, with urine,
Given as a nasal medicine, also dispel all diseases.

Camphor and turmeric,
The essence of snow,
The lotus of the padminī,
And the juice of smyungpo,
Kneaded in the palm of the Lord of Mind
And relied upon in due order,
Free one from all diseases
And fulfill every wish.
This I have declared without doubt.
It should be taken in the season of spring.

The one called Firm, greatest of the great:
The salt of her urine,
Together with her blood and her fluid,
Is mixed with mercury and honey.
These great applications
I have explained to you, lovely-faced one.
One will always be free of disease and possessed of strength.

To persons with phlegm disorders
Give it together with hot substances.
That which comes from the lotus of the short one
The wise should first collect.
With tongue and the white,
The practitioner should make pills.
By relying on them for six months
One binds both the moving and the unmoving;
As long as the body remains,
Poison will not spread.

Take the flower that comes from the Firm One,
Likewise the nectar of the crow-faced one;
The great flesh should be doubled,
Together with the mind that arises from oneself.
By eating and relying on this,
Life and health are perfected.
If one always relies on this procedure,
One will live three hundred years,
White hair and wrinkles will be removed,
And one will become fearless in all treatises.

Two zho of powdered feces,
Ashes of apāmārga,
Human urine and buffalo urine:
If the wise take these together,
Strength will arise with ease.
There is no doubt in what I have explained.
Having worshiped the vajra master,
One should thereafter undertake the rite.

Turmeric together with sugar
Removes disorders of blood and bile.

A hundred srang of woman's milk,
With a hundred srang of vajra milk,
Juice of emblic myrobalan in the same amount,
A bowlful of cow's milk,
Turmeric, honey, red sandalwood,
One srang each, and pāṭhā:
Possessed of the characteristics explained before,
A hundred and eight should be taken.
Boil it properly as before,
With white house flowers,
And it should be taken in the season of spring.

Of the five, the stout one and the rest,
I shall explain the ritual procedure of the rites.
The breastless one, the rough one,
And the leprous one, the third:
Taking the salt of her urine,
Together with blood of the thumb,
Is supreme for summoning.
Among the substances, blood of horse and of buffalo,
With blood of the wrathful one,
And likewise with blood of the youngest,
Accomplishes killing as well.
The salt of the short one,
Together with her own fluid,
And together with blood of the long one,
Brings about great increase.
That same, the ring finger, lord of the bhūtas,
Unfailingly brings under control.

Fragrant water, together with
Blood of the victory banner,
Kneaded in the palm and eaten,
Accomplishes every desired aim.
If what comes from the lotus cannot be found,
Take that which comes from a corpse's hand.
The poor one who seeks the aim of yoga
Should fulfill his wish through the offering of the hand.

Whatever is hidden in all the tantras,
All of that I have explained to you.
Having placed the empowerment in the hand,
The wise one requests the deities to depart.

The Beautiful One, with seed of kunduru,
Kneaded with a boar's tusk,
If made into a forehead mark,
Brings under control beings both moving and unmoving.

White girikarṇa
Together with priyaṅgu, licked,
Increases vitality and strength.
The eye discharge of a young maiden,
Together with realgar, if relied upon,
Destroys poisons of every kind.
The eye discharge of a dark-blue maiden,
Together with the fat of the brain:
Merely by relying on it,
White hair is destroyed without doubt.

Tongue and blood in equal parts,
With molasses and olsé, four parts in all;
Further, fluid, butter,
And mercury, taken as explained before,
Together with fine sulfur:
If relied upon, this is the supreme application.
In about six months, certainly,
One is freed from old age and sickness
And will live three hundred years.

Marrow fat and sulfur,
Half a zho of each, combined:
If relied upon for one year,
One attains the form of a god without doubt.
That which kneads the fire of the sky
Is equal in measure to susira.
This is a great application
That increases life and health.

With the calcined essence and the auspicious one,
One is endowed with the mind of a higher god.
By licking, by smelling, and by nasal medicine,
One gains a vajra body, without doubt.

Ce myang and salt, the golden tree,
And datura, all four together,
Cakramarda, powdered nakha,
Somarāja and turmeric:
Combining all these into one,
Take one pala of the substance,
And, following the rite explained before,
Cook it in clarified butter.
Aparājitā, tāla and sahadevā,
Together with the juice of fruits,
In four, five, six and seven parts:
As much as these, so much ghee.
Iron rust and priyaṅgu,
With dant, nakha and turmeric:
Cook these once more in clarified butter.
It brings clarity and increases health.
This is the rite of anointing the head.

Priyaṅgu and candra,
The flowers of kantelavana,
Dripping honey and kumuda,
Utpala and the water plant,
With barberry, this paste
Should be taken in the measure of one pala.
It kindles the fire of desire
And increases good fortune and strength.
Campaka and saffron,
With cow's bile, are rubbed on.
This is the rite of unguents.

One part of the extractor, two of clarified butter,
And three of indra should be combined.
The astringent called emblic,
Krimala and elaji,
Campaka and sandalwood,
Nāgakesara and costus,
Padma and rakta, together with pridar,
Honey-flower and priyaṅgu,
Panamala and phurmo,
Aprada together with dipuṣpa,
The water plant, one part of vīra,
Śatapuṣpā and jala, three:
Fragrant incense is supreme.
By the rite of cooking clarified butter,
Make nasal medicine, drinks and unguents.
For nasal medicine give ten pala;
For drinking, a hundred pala;
For anointing the head, twenty more;
For massage, make it three hundred pala.

Perform the rite of the decoction:
However much medicine there is,
Boil it in sixteen times as much water.
When water equal to the medicine remains,
This is taught as the best rite of decoction.

Kida and matsandi,
Sal resin and guggulu,
Kunduru and aguru,
Together with ghee: this is taught
As the best formula for incense.
Even the Thirty-Three gods are worshipped with it.
Shilajit, camphor and costus,
Sandalwood, nakha, long pepper,
And cyperus: mixed together
With the substances explained before,
With crystal sugar and with ghee,
Molasses should be left out.
In a bowl shaped like a skull cup,
Placed inside a tāla fruit:
This is the rite of the two incense devices.

Viṣṇukrānta and dasi,
Red ralpa and priyaṅgu, three,
Cinnamon, nakha and costus,
Cyperus and fragrant incense: this powder
Is the best scrub for a woman's bath.

Now, for women from whose womb
Pus, ash and the smell of fish come forth,
The way to cleanse these is explained.
First, wash with fragrant water;
Then wash with a decoction of lodhra.
Then, having placed her on a seat,
Join bowls of incense mouth to mouth
And pierce holes in them.
Inserting a tube there,
Fumigate inside the vagina.

Take a twig of the nyagrodha tree
About the size of the little finger,
And soak it in cow's urine
For the space of one day.
Having rubbed the teeth with it,
Afterward anoint the teeth
With equal parts of costus and myrobalan as a perfume.
This is best for removing foulness of the mouth.
Cyperus, costus and cardamom,
Small cardamom, coriander and licorice:
If these are eaten, they are best for taking away
The smell of pus, the smell of garlic,
And the smell of liquor.
If one soaks in and perfumes with
The two kinds of palava,
It is best for cleansing and perfuming
Flowers, sour things and the like.
Red ralpa and bāla,
Small cardamom, fragrant incense and coriander,
With nakha and cyperus,
Adding three times as much emblic:
This is the best preparation for bathing.
Cinnamon, cyperus, fragrant incense,
Patra, and kepartī's
Cyperus, with costus and nakha,
With three parts of emblic:
This is the best scrub for washing.

The external applications for kings and others:
The sequence of the sixty-four
Is mixed with the five born of oneself;
For kings and others make sixteen.
a i o a is the first.
ḷ u e ṛ is the second.
ī aṃ a o is the third.
ū ḹ ṝ ai is the fourth.
These are taught as the medicines
That make the body into a god's form.
One who knows the rite of the vowel measures
Should come to know them all.

a is the lord of the earth;
ī is sahadevā;
o is the peacock's crest;
ā is the one that brings harm;
ḹ is krita ajāli;
u is red ralpa;
e is ever-smoke;
ṝ is girikarṇikā;
ī is cakrāṅga;
aṃ is śaśiśekhara;
o is called the sun tree;
au is called lajub;
ū is called datura;
ḹ is the sprout of the head;
ṝ is buffalo horn;
e is called the Vajra Holder.

ka is guggulu. kha is the Vajra Holder. ga is datura. gha is rama. ṅa and ca are sarpodka and atapari. cha and ja are sihlā and campaka. jha is kunduru. ña and ṭa are aṅkola and sonchika. ṭha is realgar. ḍa is aguru. ḍha is sheep horn. ṇa is white dūrvā grass. ta and tha are fish and gandhika. da and dha are saffron and rocana. na is paṃśṭhi. pa and pha are sugar and clove. ba is priyaṅgu. bha is nāgakesara. ma is nakha.

The four, yar la va,
are the supreme application for wind and the rest.
pṛṅka, anjali,
red jaṭā and sahade,
made into a mark on the forehead,
bewilder the moving and the unmoving.
sutapati, mayura,
tikajala and aparājitā:
if these are smeared on the bhaga,
with a single time she becomes obedient.
vajri, meśa, aka
and adanjale are the supreme powder.
With jaṭā, katam, the maddening plant,
and pills of kanta, one brings others under control.
na, nara, anjali,
together with lajala,
make one like the Lord of the Śākyas upon water.
King, krita, anjali,
with nari and desti,
are the finest for binding fire.
bisnu, hema, vajri,
together with hlidu, if put in the mouth,
at once relieve one's weariness.
King, minister, lata
and pana are supreme for making the vajra firm.
pana, saha, śikha,
together with meśa, destroy poison.
parya, supa, meśa
and sniha, if poured into the bhaga,
bring to birth what dwells in the womb.
Buffalo horn and lata,
with nari and avana,
if smeared on the hand, make one win at wagers.
prita, candi, nara
and sahadeva, if put in the mouth,
let one roam at night without fear.
Fear, king's root and bile,
bandu and likewise minister,
putra and arthi in equal parts:
she becomes filled with desire and conceives.
śata, sringa, hasta
and powdered sandalwood
bring all sentient beings under control
and give bliss to all women.
nita, keke and peya:
if a man smears them on the bhaga,
it is supreme for bringing under one's control.
Root bile, red jaṭā,
dala, together with badu:
a pill of these, if put in the mouth,
is supreme for retaining semen in union.
mayura, aśu,
araka and meśi in powder
bring about the destruction of enemies.
Mohana, kanta, aparājitā,
joined with lord of the earth
and with bamboo seed,
are virtuous and accomplish every act.
gari, duhi, krama,
budan, cari and vajra holder:
with a paste of these mixed with one's own blood,
even a blazing axe is made like snow.
a ā i ī u ū ṛ ṝ ḷ ḹ e ai o au aṃ āḥ.
If one anoints the body with ā and the rest,
even the gods will worship one;
what need to speak of people on earth?
Further, a i o aḥ
are the application of pleasing incense.
ḹ ū ṛ au
are the finest application of rubbing ointment.
ā aṃ e ī
are the rite of eye salve and the like.
u ḷ ṛ ai
are the supreme rite of the act of bathing.
ka and the rest, with the five nectars,
are the supreme rite of bringing under control.
With ca and the rest and the five nectars,
brahmins, kṣatriyas, vaiśyas and the rest,
men and women, are brought under control.
With ṭa and the rest and the five nectars,
śūdras are brought under control.
With ta and the rest and great sesame,
made into a mark on the forehead,
the moving and the unmoving are bewildered.
With pa and the rest and the five nectars,
together with sandalwood, one brings under control.
By śa ṣa sa ha,
wind, fire, Mahendra and water,
knowing the rite of the maṇḍalas
of ya and the rest as it is,
one will perform expulsion and the rest.

This Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
was spoken by Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One (the Buddha), the Vajra Holder.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the forty-ninth chapter, the section on the application of essence extraction (rasāyana) and the sequence of the rites of the vow.

In the delight of the supreme secret,
where the self of all forever dwells,
well seated in the great secret,
there the goddess spoke these words:
"O god, the rite of the five weapons
that you taught before,
the empowerment of the garland of skulls,
and likewise the lotus grove
and the four places you taught:
what is their purpose?
How are their names in brief?
Likewise, what else is to be drawn?
The three banners that you taught:
what is the distinction of their offering?
About these I have come to doubt.
Great Sage, please explain."
"What is hidden throughout the tantras
in which all illusions play:
you who wish to ask of all this,
Great Illusion, excellent, excellent!
The vow of inner union
I shall explain in full; goddess, listen.
The set of acts, such as fitting the string,
is like gathered flowers.
Drawing it full up to the ear,
one applies the hook, the noose and the rest.
The arrow is swift and quick;
the shot is the force of the arrow.
With both right and left,
one draws full up to the eyebrow.
Holding means pausing a little;
one should shoot without overshooting.
The rite of the arrow cannot all be told.
Now I shall speak of the mudrās.
The ground is to be sealed with mudrā.
Even means the two feet side by side.
Wide means spreading them broadly.
Round is to be made like a circle.
The left slightly bent
is what is called right extended.
Left extended is, the left stretched straight,
the right slightly bent.
By the rite of the Veda of archery
all weapons are to be shot.
This is the mudrā of posture.
Now the characteristics of the body,
the mudrās, I shall fully explain."

"Taut" means not slack, that is, endowed with courage. "Steadfast" means stable and endowed with diligence. "Supple" means beautiful and speaking pleasantly. "Firm limbs" means having a disposition that greatly desires the traces of the mudrā. In "broadly filling and completely pure," "filling" means fully complete: well proportioned, with a pleasing body, smiling, and acting in harmony. "The mudrā of the body is at ease" means having a fully complete disposition.

Fever, poison and administered venom
are to be pierced by the application of meditative concentration.
Having visualized oneself in wrathful form,
making the lord of the gods (Indra) the bow,
and the nāga lord Vasuta the string,
one should imagine lightning as the arrow;
blazing flames are made the arrowhead,
and the feathers are what is called wind.
One pierces in five ways.

"The right hand" means the right nostril: from there one should emit. The left hand is the left nostril: that is withdrawing. "Having covered the target with the fist" has the meaning of bringing under control. "Drawing the bow" means summoning. Here "the fist" means consciousness and wind. "The eyes opened" means with a smiling face.

"Fixing the gaze on the fist" is the supreme means of bringing even the dead back to life.

I shall explain the characteristics of piercing,
victorious by means of bow and arrow.
The line of the eyebrows is the bow;
the reversed gaze is the arrow.
Piercing is fixing, with one's own mind,
the other's mind upon the aim.
Playful: her gait is slow;
her breasts are firm,
and their swelling is called unata.
This girl is a captivator of the mind.
All of them are the three entities.
Whatever limb is indistinct yet luminous
becomes perfectly clear and blazing.
That by which the heart holds everything
is called accomplishing the limbs.
The heart is taught to be the lotus,
and that lotus is worshiped by all.
What holds everything is the head;
from the brain it moves everywhere.
Whatever the strength of each may be,
that should be applied at that time.
The sites are taught to be of many kinds:
flesh, puskar, the bee, and so forth.
Here too, by that same sequence,
preceded by the summoning of the mind,
come one's own seat and the rest.
This is easy to understand by oneself.
I shall now state what is taught in code.
On "the square and so forth":
the square has four corners;
it is the syllable la, the maṇḍala of Mahendra.
The petaled one is the triangle,
the maṇḍala of fire, arising from raṃ.
Likewise, the fully round ones
are taught to be ba and ya.
On the one to be subdued, visualize the maṇḍalas
together with their syllables, and accomplish the rite.
Circling from the right
means visualizing all the limbs of the one to be subdued.
One should proceed step by step, pressing down.
If the practitioner, deeply absorbed,
meditates on himself as Vajrarāga,
he will enjoy every kingdom.
Again, e is made the bow,
and ba is the arrow.
Through applying the Veda of bow and arrow,
one should abide just as explained before.
The one to be subdued becomes red light;
visualize her gazing straight ahead.
The determination of the secret sites
I teach as the piercing of the flesh.
The piercing of the navel is puskara;
the heart is called the bee;
piercing the filament is the forehead.
The eyes and the rest are called the moving ones;
the host of the senses joins battle;
the lord of men is to be slain.
The instructions for all of these
should be ascertained from the guru's mouth.

On "shooting with a device and not shooting": the device is a sword, a lotus, or a wheel maṇḍala drawn together with mantras on a palm leaf, birch bark, cloth and the like. That is shooting with a device. "Not shooting" means summoning, inserting, binding and bringing under control by something that issues from the syllable at one's own heart and goes out by way of the nostrils. "Shooting by hand" means visualizing oneself as one's deity and going and coming in order to bring the pledge (samaya) under control. "Having shot, to seize again" means this: one places the target within the mudrā, raises it, and reduces it to ash. Then, with the syllables of "seize again" and the rest, one summons oneself and rises. By means of the vajra hook and the others, one summons there the wisdom that pervades every quarter of the sky like smoke, revives the target, and makes the pledge. Then, having made an effigy of the enemy and visualized Vairocana and the others as appropriate, one should meditate that they fight and bind one another, foot to foot, hand to hand, mouth to mouth, body to body, speech to speech, and gaze to gaze. This is joining battle by hand, which is of five kinds.

"Having made the vowels and consonants equal" means aṃ iṃ uṃ oṃ oṃ aṃ aḥ and ka ca ṭa ta pa ya śa.

The full application
is taught as the practice for practitioners.
Having transformed into Padmanarteśvara,
visualize him dwelling in the girl's place.
The syllable e, the syllable baṃ,
and the syllable la, the third:
here the practitioner visualizes these syllables.
Make it like the tip of a butter lamp's flame.

On "like the tip": the śikha is the head, the mass of the brain. The tip is the tip of the flame. By it everything should be melted, just as fresh butter is rendered in a pot. If one asks what result comes from doing so:

By this lamp of mantra,
in an instant she will tremble.
The girl, tormented by desire,
comes under one's control as long as she lives.
A goddess, a nāginī, or a yakṣiṇī:
on a cast image or a drawing
write her name;
the wise one places it at the heart and meditates.
Within seven days at most,
by the rite of the lamp,
it will succeed here, without a doubt.
The faculties are the substances;
deeply absorbed, accomplish the rite.
The faculties are the eye and the rest.
Reasoned application is the path.
Whatever is the nature of each,
through that very entity
accomplish it as bliss itself.
Bliss is what is called mind;
in that very thing are self, bliss, and the rest.
It is not accomplished through suffering;
it is taught to be accomplished through bliss.
The means of accomplishing supreme bliss
cannot be accomplished at all
by inferior ones who have abandoned
learning, meditative concentration and the rest.
Since such people lack skillful means,
the inferior, of bad conduct, accomplish it only with hardship.
Lacking the accomplishment of their own selves,
the unfortunate toil to no purpose,
and that is not called accomplishment.

asam means without example or equal. adhita means the union of yogins. "Oneself becomes all the buddhas" means that all things are indicated as, and enter into, nondual wisdom. pratahaṃ means each day; or else each month, that is, at the full moon; or else each year. That is, if one has not been able to gather the requisites, or lacks the opportunity, or even if time has lapsed, such people should do it each year. "Likewise" means doing it according to the rite of empowerment. Or else "likewise" refers to the words "if there is no other master, having blessed accordingly"; there, "having blessed it" means the buddha vow. Having found that, one dances together with oneself. The buddha vow is what is connected with the buddhas. Who is that? A girl perfect in form and the rest and in youth. "Dance" means that one should practice while endowed with the yoga of one's own deity. This shows the following: if the requisites such as the mudrā are not constantly available, then in order to take up that bliss again, one should make offerings by this sequence and should guard and keep the pledge. Further, the meaning taught in code will be shown: pratahaṃ means very many.

If one asks what that is, it extends as far as the words "the union of the eighteen elements." "Each month" means the moon and the red. "Each year" means from a girl of eleven years up to one of twenty-five. "If even these are not available, having blessed as appropriate" means that one should maintain the pledge alone, by oneself, through the practice of bliss. In "Through the mudrā, accomplishment will enter," "enter" means putting into practice.

In the lines

With the practitioner's left hand,
having taken hold of the lotus vessel,

the lotus vessel is a skull cup of a single piece. Here ka means the body, and pāla means protecting. "Of a single piece" means even and exceedingly beautiful. "Lotus" here means a padminī, though a girl of another class is also acceptable. The vessel is her lotus, the source of phenomena, and it is even, and so on, up to "without blemish." "Hand" is used to mean "steady." "Left" means glancing sidelong to the left. "Three banners" means the essence that drips from the girl, and that is to be consumed. In "right hand," "hand" means a girl of up to twenty-five years, and she is "right" because she is accompanied by a friend. That same one is the left, and what arises from her is to be enjoyed. As for "enjoying" it, by practicing just as the guru has shown in practice, one will attain accomplishment. The practitioner should first come to know this with certainty and only then fix the times; otherwise he will fall. "Fall" means transgressing the pledge.

The mantra is what the guru teaches;
the mudrā is what is shown to it.
By constantly joining with that very thing,
one will turn the wheel of Dharma.
All these are nonconceptual,
and yet they are not nonconceptual:
through the nature of the absence of conception,
nonconception is imputed.
Those taught for the six seasons
always abide free of conception.
As the Victors, Vajrasattva and the rest,
their nature should be fully understood.
The hot season, the rains, and autumn,
early winter, late winter, and likewise spring:
having known these as unmixed,
with this one should perform all the rites.
The hot season is Paramāśva,
likewise the rains are Heruka,
Vajrasūrya is autumn,
Padmanarteśvara is early winter,
late winter belongs to Vajrasattva,
and spring is Vairocana.
In the hot season one should worship with lamps,
likewise in the rains worship with water;
in autumn one should worship with melted butter,
and likewise in early winter offer flowers;
in late winter camphor is to be consumed,
and above all, with the four equal ingredients,
one should worship in the season of spring.
Seasonal worship is without equal;
it is the highest of all practices.
All the yoginīs,
the ḍākinīs, and the mātṛkās are pleased.
Therefore, if one worships with every effort,
one becomes the very embodiment of worship.
The yogin who has fully
become those natures
will without doubt accomplish
all the rites of practitioners.
They are the established natures,
and they are spoken of as "time."
Through the nature of time and the rest,
all the Victors truly abide.
Though all times abide in the mind,
even so there is confusion.

"Indestructible" is the syllable hūṃ, namely the hūṃ that abides in one's own body. That same syllable is called "the supreme syllable." The mudrā of that supreme syllable is the wind entering just as it is through the two doors of the nostrils, by way of the three channels, into the door of great bliss; and that same wind is the vowels and consonants, on the right and on the left. This teaches the following. Into the maṇḍala of the navel enters the wind maṇḍala of the awakening mind and so forth. Above that is the water maṇḍala, then fire, then earth, and above that the lotus. The seed that abides upon it is called "indestructible" and "the supreme syllable."

What arises from all the senses
is called the pledge skull cup.
Beauty is explained as the garland;
suchness is the crown ornament;
the crest jewel is the empowerment;
what is called "buddha" is the authorization.
This is the empowerment of Heruka.
The skull cup is called the body;
the waist bears the name "garland";
the crown ornament is called bhaga;
"buddha" is said to be the seminal fluid;
the crest jewel is explained as absent.
This is the empowerment of vajra and lotus.
I shall explain once more:
the skull cup is the pledge of the two empowerments;
the garland is called the lord of mantras;
it is marked out with sixteen lines.
Emptiness is explained as the crown ornament;
the mātṛkās, ā and the rest,
are expressed by the word "buddha."
The bliss that lies at their center
is called the crest jewel.
By all the families, in every way,
such is the empowerment, always.
What is called "glory" is joy,
and bliss is called "buddha."
The lotus is the lotus itself,
the forest is known as the dawn,
and the root is the awakening mind.
Or else the lotus is the wheel of the navel,
the forest is the mātṛkās, a and the rest,
and the root, which bestows liberation,
is by its sign the buddha mudrā.
Or else the navel is called the lake-born,
what is called the forest is speech,
the root is the vital essence of the body,
and "buddha" is the sign of mind.
"Alone" is the yoga of certainty;
"solitary" means having abandoned conception;
"hidden" means not made manifest.
The forest is the elements explained before;
because they enter together supremely,
they are spoken of as "gathered."
By the word "deity" one's own name is spoken.
To use the word agra
for planets, stars, and other such gods
is not to know the yoga.
Agra is spoken of as chief,
agra is called supreme,
agra is explained as first,
agra is what abides at the crown of the head.
Endowed with its own name,
it clears away misdeeds.
Or else the ground is a and the rest;
these are called agraha.
Abiding at the head and facing downward,
the letters rain down conventions.

"Conventions" are the vowels and consonants, by means of which one comes to live. As for "vowel," that is, svara: sva means "by oneself," and ra means "beautiful"; hence it is svara.

Ha is the letter of good fortune.
Endowed with every supreme aspect
and adorned with every ornament,
one should imagine one's own body
as Vajrasattva and the other deities.
Through this yoga of meditative concentration
one is freed from all misdeeds,
and, as one endowed with all merit,
the three things truly abide.
Whatever merit is seen in drawing it
is seen likewise in imagining it.
With this one should confer empowerment
on the great-souled disciple.
The sacred empowerment is held to be something to cultivate in meditation.
Whichever deity is supreme,
that very one the master becomes.
Whichever one the disciple is especially devoted to,
with that very one empowerment should be conferred.
Empowerment, in its four kinds,
has been explained separately again and again.
Those that were explained before
have all set it forth.
In order to realize the genuine yoga,
one should practice contrived meditation
and contrived mantra recitation.
Once the genuine yoga has been realized,
since the genuine yoga was realized
through the outer, contrived yoga,
the contrived practices are no longer to be done.
Just as one holds on to a boat
to cross to the far side of the water
and, having reached the far shore, leaves it behind,
so it is with the contrived practices.
Whatever acts, such as the maṇḍala,
one performs with a contrived mind,
because they make the outer activities clear,
are praised for beginners.
Because all those accomplishments abide here,
this is not the knowing of the Victor's very reality.
Just as the treatises on grammar
were composed by the sages,
so the contrived activities of persons
that set their own minds to work,
following after the tantras,
only stir up suffering through effort.
Therefore striving at the rites of action
such as the maṇḍala
is external and devoid of liberation;
it does nothing but afflict the person.
One does not awaken through non-conceptuality,
nor likewise through conceptuality.
If one fully knows what is utterly pure,
it becomes the very reality of all that is desired.
The intrinsic nature of cause and effect
does not exist in the ultimate sense,
yet because its nature is luminosity,
it is not emptiness either.
The aspects of every distinction of nature
abide within it
like jewels in the ocean;
its character is without beginning or end.
The world always deals in conventions;
what transcends the world runs counter to them.
What are called existence and nonexistence
are not known as the intrinsic nature itself.
One who has fully realized by this method
is not liberated if lacking completion.
Relying on the genuine path,
even if one does everything, one is liberated.
Saṃsāra and nirvāṇa,
which increase faults and good qualities,
happiness and suffering itself,
truly abide as coemergent.
Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the fiftieth chapter, the encouragement of Vajrasattva and the definitive teaching of intrinsic nature.

The Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, is complete.

It was translated and revised by the great Indian paṇḍita Gayadhara and the Tibetan chief editor and translator Gö Lhetsé, and was finalized after collation with two manuscripts.

Translator's notes

In the line on the sense bases, the text reads "ba de ba'i mchog"; this is taken as an evident slip for "bde ba'i mchog," "supreme in bliss," the refrain used throughout. The names of the five element goddesses (Pātanī, Māraṇī, Ākarṣaṇī, Narteśvarī, Mahāmāyā) are identified from the standard Tibetan renderings of this well-known set. "The Goer" translates the Tibetan "'gro ma" literally; its Sanskrit form is not certain. "Kalpa" renders རྟོག་པ, which here seems to play on both senses of the Sanskrit kalpa, a ritual system and an age ("in time past ... the first kalpa"). "The one who steals the union of all the buddhas" is a literal rendering of an obscure phrase. In the description of the faces, "the right is not white" is literal; presumably a dark (blue or black) face is meant. ཉེ་བར་བ་རྒྱན was read as ཉེ་བར་བརྒྱན "adorned", and སྒོ་མ་བྱེད as སྒོམ་བྱེད "meditating." In the final verse, "hair, moon and the rest, the things that follow" (སྐྲ་ཟླ་ ... རྗེས་དངོས) is uncertain; it may be coded tantric language or a corruption (for example སྒྲ "sound" for སྐྲ "hair"), so it is translated literally. Mother, sister and so on, the meats, and the substances of the five nectars are conventionally read in the commentarial tradition as coded references to consorts, sacramental substances and bodily fluids; they are translated as they stand. "The Goer" here renders ཀུན་ཏུ་འགྲོ་བ; it may instead mean "beings" ("one should always honor beings") or an epithet such as "the All-Pervading One"; the reading as the consort follows the earlier use of the Goer. In "When it is shaken by suffering, it goes to utter cessation," the subject is understood as the bliss just mentioned. The mantra section is a coded description of letters; the lines are rendered literally without attempting to resolve the code. The verses from "That which ends the karma accumulated before" to "who grants every accomplishment" are a coded extraction of the mantra (mantroddhāra), in which letters are indicated by names such as "the last letter after sa," "fire," "wind," "arrows" and "crescent moon and dot." These have been translated as they stand without decoding. In "is called trāsaya and is uttered," the text reads brdzun ("false"), which does not fit; it is taken as a slip for brjod ("utter"). The term gshor, here "an ornament," is unclear. The phrase byas pa chung gsan pa, rendered "dulling the hearing," is also uncertain. The consort's name dga' mdzes ma has been translated as "Lovely Joy" because the Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. Rig pa bzhi may also refer to four vidyā goddesses rather than "four knowledges." The term rendered "tamers" (Tibetan 'dul byed) stands in a list of spirit classes beside vināyakas; its Sanskrit equivalent is uncertain, so it is translated literally. "Rejoicing in the freedom from desire of all the tathāgatas" renders an awkward phrase (dgas pa) whose exact sense is unclear. In the visualization, the Tibetan reads zhal bzhin sa, evidently a slip for zhal bzhi nas, "from his four faces," which is how it is translated. "Fine flour" renders zhib ma; the quarter of the wind is traditionally the northwest. "The nāga Śeṣa" takes the Tibetan klu lhag ma can as a rendering of Śeṣa. "The water streaming from the bodies" renders an obscure phrase, literally "the water arising from the ladle of the body"; the sense is uncertain. In "the bound mudrā of Vajrahūṃkāra" the text reads "hundred hands" (phyag brgya), evidently a slip for "mudrā" (phyag rgya). The names of the cloud kings are translated literally because their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence; the list as written seems to contain more than eight names. "The seed syllable of Moja" is uncertain and may refer to a plant (mocā) rather than a deity. "Steeps it with roots until it is sour" is a tentative rendering. "Caṇḍa" renders gtum po, "the Fierce One," which may also be read as an epithet rather than a name. "Summoning someone in bodily form" renders the obscure gong bu dgug pa, literally "summoning the lump"; it may refer to drawing the target bodily to the effigy. "Nail dye" (sen rtsi) and "clematis" (lce myang tshar) are tentative identifications of these substances. "Indra's hand" translates dbang po'i lag pa, the medicinal plant dbang lag. "The Great Wise Lady" renders བློ་གྲོས་ཆེན་མོ; it may be a goddess's name or an address, and the referent is unclear. "Those who dwell in the sky" reads མཁར་གནས as མཁའ plus the locative ར. སྔགས་འདི་བདུན could also be read as "these seven mantras". The identities of the "vajra-toothed creature" (རྡོ་རྗེ་སོ་ཅན) and the "mad yakṣa" (གནོད་སྦྱིན་སྨྱོན་པ, possibly a plant or substance name) are uncertain. The material སྤྲ་ཚིལ is taken as "monkey fat", and རོའི་སྐེ as a cord from a corpse; both are tentative. The seed syllable described as "the last of a class on the seat of ra, with the fourth vowel and bindu" is left as the text describes it. "Noose of the heart mantra" renders a terse phrase that may also mean simply "the noose of the heart." The word rendered "interspersed" after "letters of the name" on the palm leaf is unclear in the Tibetan and may conceal a seed syllable. Several materia medica are uncertain: "spunpa" is left untranslated as unidentified; "the lord of disease" probably stands for costus (kuṣṭha); "the cooker" is translated literally because it cannot be identified with confidence. "The ten substances" renders "the substances of half of twenty," presumably a set named elsewhere in the tantra. "Fish bowl" translates the term literally; the exact kind of vessel is unclear. "His sides adorned by Mahāmāyā" renders the obscure word thong ka, whose meaning is uncertain. "Twenty-four ḍākas in a half ring" renders nyi shu bzhi phyed bskor, which could also be read as a number ("twenty-three and a half") and is unclear. "Horse dung" renders rta bla mo, an uncertain term that may stand for rta lci. Kusumja is kept as written; the flower intended is probably the safflower (kusumbha). The mantra-like verses describing the arrangement of syllables (tortoise seat, Yama, Nairṛti, Varuṇa and so on) are an encoded syllable-code; I have translated them as they stand without attempting to decode them. A few substances (manada, nyanpa, dumabu) are untranslated, being unidentified. The line "wheel of the opposing side" renders ཕ་རོལ་གྱི་འཁོར་ལོ literally. "Antimony" renders ལིག་བུ་མིག, an uncertain mineral or eye-pigment term. The simile "clear like the finest flower of the house," the "seven shadows," and the phrase rendered "one laid out before one" are obscure in the Tibetan and are translated literally. Here རྡུལ is taken in its tantric sense of menstrual blood (rajas). Rocana is gorocanā, a bezoar-like substance. In the sign-language verses, ལིང་ག is the phallus, not an effigy. འཕྲོག་བྱེད་མ is taken as Hāritī, though it could mean seizing spirits in general. The Tibetan gloss for mutaka is the rare word klum, which looks like a slip for klu mo, "nāginī." It is translated that way, but the reading is uncertain.

In the yantra instructions, terms such as Piercing, Dream, Moon, Tortoise, Delusion, Excellent, Increase and Destroyer of Yama appear to be coded names for letters or diacritics. They are rendered literally, because the text does not state their values here.

"Touching the empty place" renders stong pa as written. Its intended bodily referent is unclear. This passage encodes the letters of a mantra grid in a coded alphabet: "middle," "end," "outer of the wind," "tortoise," "drop" (likely the anusvāra), "dream," "moon," "awl," "makara," "increase," "delusion," "neuter" and the like designate specific letters, vowel signs or positions in the directional petals of a letter-wheel. Here the word glossed elsewhere as Madhyamaka and "extreme" is a code term and is rendered "middle" and "end." The key to the code is not given in this piece, so the terms are translated literally. "Liberating" refers to the ritual act of forcibly liberating, that is, slaying. The numbering of cells as written skips the twenty-fifth in the first sequence. This is translated as it stands. This passage encodes mantra syllables in a grid of numbered squares using code words (the outer of the wind, tortoise, dream, sun and moon, awl, makara-rider, delusion, mountain, Excellent, the southern end, the middle, and so on). These are translated literally, since their letter values are not given here. "The middle" here renders དབུ་མ as a position in the letter-grid, not the Madhyamaka school. ལྟོ in the thirty-eighth square is read as ལྷོ, "south", in line with the parallel phrases. The text gives "forty-second square" twice in a row, and the first may be an error for the forty-first. The sense of "a hero's freshly made vase" is uncertain. The first part of this passage is a coded prescription for filling the squares of a yantra with mantra syllables. Words such as Wind, Mountain, Tortoise, Dream, Moon, Piercer, Increase, Delusion and Excellent are code names for letters and vowel signs, and "first," "middle," "last," and the directions refer to positions in the arrangement of the alphabet. They are translated literally, as the code is not resolved in the text itself. The list at the end gives such code names for the vowels in two sequences. The square numbers follow the text exactly, including "the fourteenth" in a sequence otherwise running through the thirties and forties, and the repeated "thirty-fifth." The "night of the bhūtas" is presumably the fourteenth of the dark fortnight. The total of 21,600 breaths per day and night is the standard tantric reckoning. The twelve names in this rotating table are names of astrological days. I have translated their meanings (Lord of the Earth, Increase, Delusion, Excellent, Bull, Tortoise, Makara, Widow, Birthmark, Piercing, Wandering, Dream) because I cannot identify Sanskrit originals for them with confidence. Makara stands for the Tibetan "water monster." Two phrases are uncertain. The phrase rendered "will hold the base in esteem" may instead mean "will be counted among the lowly." In the description of the child born on the day of Piercing, the phrase rendered "will cause its father and mother to go hungry" literally says "father and mother hungry," and its exact sense is unclear. The names of the days (Mole, Widow, Makara, Tortoise, Bull, Excellent, Delusion, Increase, Lord of the Earth, Dream, Wavering, Piercing) are translated from the Tibetan, since their Sanskrit originals cannot be identified with confidence. The text has ན་དབུ་ཅན, which is unclear; it is read here as "sickly," on the assumption that ན་བ་ཅན was meant. In the sentence on the Dream day, ནམ་ཤི་ན is read as ན་མ་ཤི་ན, "if one does not die within," to match the parallel sentences. The day and night lengths are given in nāḍikās (Tibetan chu tshod, units of twenty-four minutes, sixty to a day and night). "Six plus two tens" and similar expressions render the Tibetan "six and ten multiplied by two." The paired figures for day and night always add up to sixty. In one line, "four plus three tens" for the southward course, the word "ten" is implied by the pattern. In the final sequence the text has twenty-four for the day where the pattern would lead one to expect twenty-six; it is translated as written. The phrase "white as snow, the kunda flower and the moon" reads the Tibetan "gang" as "gangs," snow, a common simile for whiteness. "The two sky-goers" probably means the sun and moon, the heavenly bodies, rather than ḍākas. The vowel divination lists the vowels in order, so the second and eighth entries, written simply as "a," stand for the long vowel and the diphthong. Several technical lines on the timing method, such as "lords of speech," "lords of the earth" and "the two neuters," are obscure and have been translated literally. This passage names a cycle of twelve divisions (with Increase and Rohitā alternating as the first) used for divination. The Tibetan sometimes translates and sometimes transliterates the same name (for example Tortoise and Kūrma, Bull and Vṛṣabha, Excellent and Bhadra, Delusion and Mohitā, widow and Raṇḍā, "the agitated one" and Vyākulī, "the piercing one" and Bhidrikā); each has been given one consistent form. The division rendered "Mikirā" appears in the Tibetan both as a transliteration and as "mole" (smé ba); its identification is uncertain. The phrase rendered "relief will come" (skya bseng) is obscure. The divination terms "ear rings" render the Tibetan "ear weeps"; "twitches" in the whole-body sign reads the written "left" as "moves." The twelve names opening the passage (a correspondence of goddesses with nāga names, continuing the previous piece) are obscure; several are translated by meaning because they could not be confidently identified: Yoke-Trained, Endowed with Knowledge, Mole, Piercer, Roamer, Goer by Mind, Clenched. The astrological instructions on time reckoning ("multiplied threefold," "four quarters," "cutting of the knot") are rendered literally. ཆད་པ in the list of divinatory topics is taken as "punishment" rather than the doctrinal "annihilation," which does not fit this context. གོང་བུ ("ball, lump") is rendered as piṇḍa, and གཤོར as "basin"; both are uncertain. The line "first, having meditated on the corpse" renders རོ, which could also mean "taste" (as in "one taste"). The north-quarter implement རྡེའུ་ཆུང literally means "small pebble"; it is rendered "small drum" on the assumption that a ḍamaru is meant, which is uncertain. The Ganges is spelled irregularly in both places (གདྷཱ, གདྒཱ) and is read as Gaṅgā. The name read as Suravairin is written ལྷའི་སྒྲ ("sound of the gods"), which makes no sense as this hero's name; the parallel lists of the twenty-four heroes indicate ལྷའི་དགྲ ("enemy of the gods", Suravairin), a near-identical spelling, and that reading was followed. The yoginī rendered "the Protectress" (སྐྱོང་མ) occupies the place of Saṃcālinī in the usual list of six; the Tibetan word does not match that name, so it is translated literally.
