The liṅga in the opening lines is the sexual organ in a completion-stage visualization, not an effigy. Himālaya's substance, literally "the furrow of the mtsho ga ma," is obscure; rendered as "the parting of the crown," following the parallel Cakrasaṃvara list. "Feather tuft" renders sgro chun literally; its sense in "producing the honey" is unclear. Suravairin for lha'i dgra ("enemy of the gods") is a reconstruction. The names of the paired heroes and goddesses follow the parallel Cakrasaṃvara lists. In that list the Tibetan "Earth Protectress" (sa srung ma) corresponds to Airāvatī. "The day of the bhūtas" and "the night of the bhūtas" most likely refer to the fourteenth of the dark fortnight (bhūtacaturdaśī). "Lord of the Hooded Ones" translates the wheel's Tibetan name, meaning lord of serpents or nāgas; no Sanskrit form is reconstructed. The line rendered "Then, taking up this song" is obscure; the Tibetan word can also mean "neck." The phrase "cili as many as there are" in the rākṣasa direction is unclear in the Tibetan. "Saṅkoṭa" is given as the Tibetan spells it; the plant is uncertain (possibly aṅkoṭa). In the first sentence the text reads "bde" where the sense requires "de" ("that one"); translated as "that person." "Preta" (literally hungry ghost) in these ritual ingredients evidently means the dead or a corpse, as the parallel "nail-dye of a corpse" shows; the exact substance meant by "nail-dye" (sen rtsi) is uncertain. "With this Blessed One" may alternatively be an address to the Blessed One. "Lord of Heroes" translates an epithet whose Sanskrit form (perhaps Vīreśvara) is not certain. Ba bla is rendered generically as "yellow pigment"; it is a yellow coloring substance whose exact identification varies. Sen rtsi is taken as lac dye. The phrase describing the vajra as "six-faced" (kha drug pa) is literal and its precise iconographic sense is unclear. "Great goddess" is read as a vocative, consistent with the later address "fair-faced one." "Aricataka" is a guess at a substance name that I could not identify. In the next line, "turmeric" reads ཡུང་བདག as a form of ཡུང་བ. "Vajrasattva is overwhelmed" renders བརླག་འགྱུར ("is destroyed, lost") literally. The sense is presumably that the deity is compelled or overpowered, but the reading is uncertain. "Royal application" for རྒྱལ་གྱི་སྦྱོར་བ is also uncertain; it could mean "the application of victory." The Compendium of Reality and the Assembly are taken to be the Tattvasaṃgraha and the Guhyasamāja. ཉོན་མོངས is rendered "afflicted" for consistency with the glossary. In context it may mean "toil fruitlessly," in contrast to succeeding "without affliction" through mudrā. This is an alchemical and medical passage full of rare drug names and code terms ("hero," "twice-born," "elephant" and "lion" cooking, "the maiden"). Many cannot be identified with confidence and are given as transliterations or literal renderings: sbu gu'i zhag ("fat of the hollow reed"), so ("tooth"), lag skyes ("nail perfume," taken as karaja/nakha), so 'cha' ("chewing stick"), cawa (bca' ba), dowa (do ba), the descriptive plant names, garra, sāliga, "sage's son," atrapati, riso, bardar, kasi, sirisaku, poson, zhiga and zhiga-jeu. Lhang tsher is taken as sulphur. Rang skyur is taken as sour gruel. The "heart and eye" line and the line on "making hair grow on the head" are interpretive. Lhangtser is left untranslated; in this alchemical context it is a mineral paired with mercury, probably sulphur or a similar substance, but the identification is uncertain. "The sharp substance" renders rnon po, likely Skt. tīkṣṇa (steel or iron) in rasāyana usage. "The Powerful One" (stobs ldan) is used both of mercury and of the consort woman whose description follows; the text moves between the two. Several plant and mineral names (candra wood, mahāhata, mokṣa, bartaki, kuba, sindubari, bandu, karanada, olse) are given as transliterated and could not be identified with confidence. The processes purifying, stupefying and killing correspond to the classical rasāyana operations śodhana, mūrchana and māraṇa. Most of the goddesses, kṣetrapālas and sources in this list are given in Tibetan translation, and their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence; they are rendered literally in English (Peaceful Face, Great Bell, Fire Face, Great Ears, Grandmother, Tawny Red One, Donkey Rider, Cow Ear, and so on). "Vāñcala" follows the Tibetan spelling and may stand for Pañcāla. The weapon "ga ya" is taken as gadā, "mace." "The Great Cleft" (dbye chen) may be the name of a tree or site; its sense is uncertain. Several goddess and kṣetrapāla names in this list (Hair-Given, Bright-Faced, Lightning-Face, Fire-Tongue-Face, Great Sound, Generator, Terror, Great Observance, Enjoyment, Lord of Men, the Empowering One, the Desirous One, and others) could not be identified with certainty and are translated by meaning. The place names Soba, Kṣereker, Putanara and the names Amrake and Janasa are given as the Tibetan spells them, since they could not be identified confidently. "Club" renders the Tibetan "ga ya", read as gadā, which is uncertain; "jila" is kept as a sound word. In the invocation list, several names of goddesses are rendered in their probable Sanskrit forms (Indrāṇī, Candrī, Lambā, Hārītī, Dīpā). The exact reading of "the dwellings of beings" is uncertain. The names rendered "the Sucking One" and "the Blazing One" (and "the Lamp Goddess" in the earlier list, possibly the same figure as the Blazing One) are translated literally, since their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. "Day of the bhūtas" likely means the fourteenth lunar day. "The great oil" and "the great incense" are tantric code terms, commonly understood as human fat and a substance of human origin. The implement read as "triple banner" and the "plowshare brimming with blood" are translated as the text stands. "Placed it inside a horn" renders a phrase meaning literally "that which grows on top"; a horn seems the most likely referent, but a skull is also possible. "Youthful" reads gzhon pa for the written bzhon pa ("mount"), which does not fit. The goddesses called here the Blazing Lady, the Lamp Lady, the Fierce Lady, Vajrā and the Sucking Lady are rendered from their Tibetan meanings, since their Sanskrit names cannot be fixed with confidence; the Lamp Lady is probably the same figure as the Blazing Lady. "Jayā" takes the Tibetan "Victory and so forth" as the lunar-day class Jayā. "The day of the bhūtas" probably means the fourteenth lunar day. "The Sucking One" translates the Tibetan name of a female spirit; its Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. "The Ravisher" likewise renders a descriptive epithet, apparently of Kambojī. The line about her being drawn on the hide reads more naturally if the verb is "draw" (one would expect a different spelling), so it is rendered as "have the Ravisher drawn upon it". In Śaṅkara's description, the text has "garland of what is obtained," which is evidently a slip for "garland of skulls," and it is translated that way. The implement held in his remaining hand, written gshor, is unidentified and is left as "shor". The Tibetan name rendered Vāsudeva (literally "wealth god") is identified from his attributes, the wheel and club, and from the mantra naming Nārāyaṇa. Mount Kailāsa renders the Tibetan Tisé. Numbers in the lifespan section are given, as in Indian astronomical convention, by symbolic words, kept here as in the text: the three qualities (guṇas) stand for three, the Vedas for four, the suns for twelve, the vowels for sixteen, and "the times" probably for three or six. "The King of Obstructors" is Vighnarāja, likely a form of Gaṇapati. The text reads "two hundreds" (brgya phrag gnyis) where the sequence of days (fifteen, then seventeen) requires sixteen; I have read "twice eight" (brgyad phrag gnyis). The body part written bzla is taken as brla, "thighs". "Embodied beings die within six and two" is cryptic in the Tibetan and rendered literally. The text uses Indian number symbolism: "the Vedas" stands for four, "the sun" for twelve, and "the times" (rendered "the seasons") most likely for six, which fits the decreasing series of lifespans given for twenty-two to twenty-five days. The portion numbers stand between the signs, and the genitive construction suggests each number belongs to the sign that follows it; the order of the Tibetan has been kept. "The lord of beings" probably denotes death or Yama. In "the channel situated at the throat" the Tibetan has an unclear syllable (blo) after "throat", which is left untranslated. "Lord of Wisdom" takes ye shes dbang as a vocative epithet of the Buddha. "The swan" (haṃsa) refers to the vital breath or consciousness drawn out through the right, solar channel. The line rendered "the ears, the gods who are siddhas" is one syllable short in the Tibetan as written; it is read as listing the ears as an exit door leading to the realm of siddha gods. In "imagine the white seed of water" the text reads a syllable short; the evident reading is "seed of water" (ས་བོན), as in the following line's "seed of fire." In the chapter on transference, the "door of becoming," "water door" and "non-water door" are the lower bodily apertures (genital, urinary and anal). The names "the Blazing One," "the Sucking One" and "the Robber of the Wise" are translated from their Tibetan meaning because their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. The hand signs at the opening are labeled with Sanskrit words in Tibetan letters, given here as spelled. The line under soma, "the thumb, the wrathful one, eating, and so on," is terse and its sense is uncertain.

The vowels are paired with the planets by traditional epithets. "The Crooked One" is taken as Mars (Skt. vakra), "born of the Moon" as Mercury, "guru of the gods" as Jupiter, "son of the Sun" as Saturn, and "the hare-marked one" as the Moon.

"Bhadrā day" renders བྷ་དྲ་ཡི་ཉི་མ. It may refer to a class of lunar dates, or it may be the day associated with the Ram, whose entry the passage otherwise lacks. In the middle-winter verse no zodiac sign is named.

"Tenth" renders གཉིས་ལྔ, literally "two-five." The lunar mansion གྲོ་ཞུན is taken as Śravaṇa.

The line "is therefore declared to be called 'Nam'" (དེས་ནམ་ཞེས་བྱ་བར་བརྗོད) is obscure. "Nam" may be a slip for a word meaning "mother." The address "O Mahāmāyā" in the second line renders a slightly different Tibetan epithet ("great magical display") from the usual one. It is taken to be the same goddess, who is addressed as Mahāmāyā later in this passage.

Several of the twenty-four goddesses' names could not be securely identified with Sanskrit forms and are translated: "She Beyond Old Age," "She of Fierce Form," "the Hostile One," "the Pendent One," "the Snatcher," and "the Sucker." Karālī, Sthūlakālī and Cāndrī are probable reconstructions.

Many words in the calculation passages ("earth," "sun," "eye," "moon," "arrow," "burner," "taste," "faculties," "direction," "quality," "lunar day") are almost certainly symbolic number words in the Indian manner (for example, eye for 2, arrow for 5, taste for 6). They are translated literally because the intended values and procedure cannot be reconstructed with certainty. The phrases "thirteen and three" and "the lives of the drops" are likewise obscure. The time units ཆ་ཤས, ཡུད་ཙམ, ཐང་ཅིག and ཆུང་ཟད are rendered with neutral English terms ("portion," "brief moment," "short spell," "little while"). The text equates the nāḍikā with a ཡུད་ཙམ even though the same word was used earlier for a tiny unit. That reading is kept as written. སྲང is rendered pala and ཆུ་ཚོད nāḍikā. འོལ་སེ is a small weight unit whose Indian equivalent is uncertain, so it is left as "olsé." The lunar mansions are given their standard Sanskrit names. The human group lists Mṛgaśiras a second time, after the god group, as the Tibetan has it. The Tibetan འདྲེན་མ for the third class of zodiac signs is rendered by its astrological sense, "dual." The opening lines are taken as the Goddess's question, followed by the Blessed One's reply. Rohita appears to be the name of a particular day or period and is left as a name. The astrological terms are rendered by their standard Tibetan equivalents: the lunar mansions Maghā, Ārdrā, Bharaṇī, Pūrvaphalgunī, Śatabhiṣaj, Āśleṣā, Pūrvabhādrapadā, Pūrvāṣāḍhā and Kṛttikā, and the planets Mars, Saturn, Jupiter ("guru of the gods") and Venus. The term rendered "Mercury" is unusual and the identification is probable but not certain. "The minister of the Mighty One" is translated literally; it may designate Venus (Śukra, preceptor of Bali), but this is uncertain. "Lord of the Earth" as the name of a day is translated literally because its Sanskrit form could not be identified. The directional names follow the usual guardians: Yama for the south, the rākṣasas for the southwest, the waters for the west, the Terrifying One for the northwest, treasure for the north, and the Three-Eyed One for the northeast. The lines on "aspect" are astrological and remain obscure. The line "others become clear, and others' forms and the rest, the central ones" is obscure in the source. The verse on killing father and mother, seizing the brahmins and destroying the king is antinomian tantric language, which the commentarial tradition reads symbolically, for example as cutting off ignorance and craving and so on. It is not a literal instruction. "The stationary ones" assumes the word is to be read as "stable, immobile" (sthāvara), referring to plants and the like; as written it means "the dependent ones." "Ratnasambhava" renders the Tibetan "Ratna" (Jewel) in the list of the five buddhas on the prongs; the full name is supplied from context. "The Exhorter" translates a goddess name whose Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. Several measurement verses of the vajra (the lion's nose, the twenty-seven parts) are technical and obscure; they are rendered literally. The Tibetan "half-nets" reads a slightly irregular spelling as the evident word for "half". "Beautiful Hue, Honey, Lovely Lady, and Jewel Coil" are taken as the names of the remaining four of the eight goddesses. The Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence, so they are translated literally. "Shoes" reads ལྷ་མ as ལྷམ. In "band of vajra garlands," སྐྲ་རགས is read as སྐ་རགས, and in "five fingers," ལྒ is read as ལྔ. Read as written, these would give "hair," and ལྒ makes no sense. The monk "in his formal standing" (དངོས་གནས) is an uncertain phrase. It apparently refers to a monk bound by monastic status, who is unsuited to receive these teachings. The line rendered "In the enjoyment of the lord of joy" is short by a syllable and its sense is unclear; "lord of joy" may refer to a deity or to the master of a feast. "Supreme bliss" translates a term that can also be read as the deity name Saṃvara; the context favors the descriptive sense here. The antinomian injunctions ("You should kill living beings," and so on) are intentionally paradoxical tantric statements, traditionally read in an interpretive, non-literal sense. The verse at the start of the thirty-fourth chapter is terse. "Variegated" renders a word that commonly stands for the crossed or variegated vajra (viśvavajra), and "the indestructible and auspicious" seems to name what is drawn at its center, perhaps a seed syllable. The reading is uncertain. "Wisdom, perfect enjoyment, and emanation" appear to allude to the wisdom (dharma) body, the enjoyment body and the emanation body. "The Beautiful One" renders མཛེས་མ, one of four yoginīs listed with Ḍākinī, Khaṇḍarohā and Rūpiṇī; in the Cakrasaṃvara set the corresponding figure is Lāmā, but the Tibetan here is not the usual rendering of that name, so it is translated literally. In chapter 36, "three-flag gesture" renders བ་དན་གསུམ་པ, likely the tripatākā hand gesture. Several technical terms of archery in chapter 37 (ལྟོང་རྒྱུད, སྲན་འཛུགས) are obscure and rendered tentatively. In the closing prose, "bhūta tree" renders འབྱུང་པོའི་ཤིང, probably a plant name (bhūtavṛkṣa), and གན་དུ་ཤ is an unidentified ingredient given in phonetic form. "Pushel tse" is given in phonetics because the plant could not be securely identified. "Upright Hero" is a literal rendering of an unidentified plant name. "Golden tree" may stand for kanaka, that is, the thorn apple (dhattūra), but this is not certain. The line rendered "from the tantra of unfailing discipline I have explained the retention" is obscure; "retention" probably refers to holding back the seminal fluid. In "charge the lords," the Tibetan word for "lords" may refer to the guardian deities, or may be a code term for a substance; the line is uncertain. The "bhūta day" of the waning fortnight is normally the fourteenth. Here liṅga and bhaga are the male and female organs in a context of sexual yoga, not the effigy of a wrathful rite. The "messengers" (dūtī) of the colophon are the female consorts. "Do it no harm" reads the written མི་སྣོད as མི་གནོད. "Having split the back of its neck" reads གཤེགས as གཤགས. "Solitary liṅga" for མཚན་མ་གཅིག / མཚན་གཅིག is a tentative interpretation; the phrase may refer to some other single mark or sign. "The spinning wheel" (འཁོར་བའི་འཁོར་ལོ) is taken as a revolving wheel such as a potter's wheel rather than "the wheel of saṃsāra," and "turning it back" is literal. "The day of the bhūtas" is taken as the fourteenth lunar day. Several plant names (Paṇḍaraka, Veti, Biṭhṇubi, kagagaja, Bhramadanta, "the golden tree," "the forest garland") could not be identified with confidence and are given as they appear or translated literally. "Vajra water" is a tantric code term, most often understood as urine. "The bhūta day of the fortnight of the pretas" likely means the fourteenth day of the dark fortnight. The "water-wheel of the she-camels" is a tentative reading of an obscure phrase. "Fifth earth-boundary" and "seed syllable of wind" appear to be coded mantra syllables; the exact reference is unclear. The tree name rendered "surakta" and the bird rendered "so bird" could not be identified with confidence. Two different Tibetan names for the deity who destroys obstructors are both rendered Vighnāntaka. "A single citron placed in the mouth turns one into a citron" follows the Tibetan literally; the intended effect (perhaps concealment of form) is unclear. Several plant names could not be securely identified and are given in phonetic form: kubakhande (perhaps a gourd, Tibetan ku ba), olmasé and olsé (probably the same plant, spelled two ways), and kokanata (possibly kokanada, red water lily). "Golden tree" translates gser shing literally; it may denote dhattūra (kanaka), but this is uncertain. "Five products of the cow" reads ba for the written pa in pa yi rnam lnga, which otherwise makes no sense. Gsal shing is taken as an impaling stake. "Indra's weapon" probably means the rainbow, as Sanskrit indrāyudha does. In the line "its forehead parted from its body," the forehead and body seem to describe parts of the written letter's shape; this reading is uncertain. The coloring substances "cokla" and "lhambu" (Tibetan cog la ma, lham bu) could not be identified with confidence and are kept as transliterations. The verse about "the vine" and "the line of the breath" is obscure; it is translated literally. "Ears" renders rna bdag, which is read as rna ba dag. The phrase rendered "like glistening moisture" (ལྷམ་བུ་ཆུ་གཤེར་ལྟ་བུ) is unclear; ལྷམ་བུ is not a recognizable term here, and only the sense of moisture is secure. "The twofold mark" (ཆ་གཉིས་པ) for the letter cha probably refers to the two dots of the visarga, but this is not certain. Throughout, "the target" renders བསྒྲུབ་བྱ, the person on whom the rite is performed, and "subjugation" renders དབང in its ritual sense. The passage works through the letters of the Sanskrit alphabet. In two places the letter named in the heading does not match the letter then described. The text speaks of the action of the final letter after "dha" and then describes ṇa; it apparently means the letter closing the ṭa row, after ḍha. It also announces the action of "dha" and then describes bha. Both are translated as written. The "emptying action" of da renders སྟོང་པའི་ལས literally; its effect in the verses is paralysis and immobilization, so the term may stand for a stopping or paralyzing rite. "Indra's weapon" renders དབང་པོའི་མཚོན་ཆ, which may refer to the thunderbolt or to the rainbow. The passage describes the rites associated with the labial consonants pa, pha, ba, bha and ma, followed by ya and ra; the final two letters introduced, associated with Mahendra (the earth element) and water, are la and va, as the next piece confirms. "Kusuma fluid" is left as kusuma ("flower"), a tantric code term often taken to mean menstrual blood. The line placing the target at the center of ba contains an unclear syllable (sgrub pos par bsam); it is rendered as "the practitioner should imagine." "The syllable of water" reads ཅུ as ཆུ, "water," which suits the context (the preceding verses announce Mahendra and water, and va is the fourth of the semivowels ya, ra, la, va). "Master of poetry" reads སྙན་དང་གས as སྙན་ངག, "poetry." The line rendered "For women and for lords" (བུད་མེད་དབང་ཕྱུག་རྣམས་ལ) could also be understood as "toward the sovereign goddesses." The four goddesses are identified with the standard Yamadāḍhī, Yamadūtī and Yamadaṃṣṭrī, and Yamamathanī follows in the next piece. The coded syllables in the list of nectar substances spell out, in sequence, the familiar line beginning "rahasye parame ramye," which is why the text afterward refers to "'Secret' and the rest"; each syllable is assigned a ritual substance. In "se is the essential leaf," the Tibetan word rendered "essential" is unclear in this context and the substance cannot be identified with confidence. "Saṃvara Uttara" renders a Tibetan phrase that may instead mean simply "the supreme Saṃvara"; the reference is uncertain. The closing verses and prose gloss the tantra's opening line about "the reality of all ḍākas, the being." The commentary maps the opening words of the sūtra formula, "thus have I heard at one time," onto beginning, end, middle, and "rightly abiding." The phrase rendered "roll it with the seed" reads bon as written; it is taken as a slip for sa bon, "seed." The quoted phrase rendered "sources of relief" (dbugs 'byung) is uncertain in this context. "Fair-Faced One" renders bzhin mdzes ma, an epithet of the goddess addressed. "Brought to its final culmination" interprets the unusual phrase མཐར་ཐུག་པར་མོན་པ. "Up to twenty-five" at the end is left as stated because the text does not say what is being counted. The "long and so forth" probably refers to long vowels and the like, in connection with the vowels and consonants mentioned in the verse. "Seated upon a messenger" renders ཕོ་ཉ (dūta) literally; in this ritual context it may refer to a ritual partner or a corpse seat, but the text does not specify. "Great flesh" is the standard tantric term for human flesh. Ten million leagues renders "a hundred hundred-thousands of yojanas." The line describing the skull reads "beautiful in form, unbroken, not clear" and is one syllable short. "Clean" is a tentative rendering of the last element.

"The day of Bhūta" most likely refers to the lunar day sacred to the bhūtas, usually the fourteenth of the dark fortnight. "Great red" renders dmar chen, a coded term for a transgressive substance, probably blood or human flesh. "Lungtang" is an unidentified plant whose seeds are used for rosaries, here left in phonetic form. "Lapis" for mu men is a probable identification of this precious stone. Lalanā and rasanā are the left and right subtle channels, here equated with the two ladles of the fire offering. The line rendered "the one who must be led as one who must be led" is obscure in the Tibetan; the sense of the third category of disciple is uncertain. "Spread out the great royal enclosure" is a tentative rendering of an unclear line. "Gabakṣa" is kept as spelled because the substance cannot be confidently identified. The "hawk-faced one and the others" are the wrathful goddesses emanated as ritual stakes (kīla), single-pointed below the navel, to pin down the obstructors. The text reads "wish to ask" in both requests; in this rite for drawing the maṇḍala lines, the evident reading is "wish to draw," and it has been translated so. The word rendered "portico" is uncertain in its precise architectural sense. In the line "Ḍākinī and the others on the petals," the term elsewhere given as "ḍāka" is rendered Ḍākinī, since it names the first goddess of the petal circle. The bare "Secret" in the list of tantras is taken as the Guhyasamāja. This chapter is an alchemical (rasāyana) section; many plant and mineral names are given in Tibetan transliteration of Sanskrit or in obscure Tibetan terms (janita, binjara, campa, mahāhahā, makṣa, bartaki, kuba sindhuvara, atrapata, rakhu, kirisopa, shingsho, sopañcana, nonpo). These are rendered as they stand because they cannot be identified with confidence. Lhang tsher is taken as mica (abhraka), which fits the four classical varieties described (peacock-neck, snake, frog, cloud-like). "Blue elixir" renders bcud len sngon po; it might instead mean "the former elixir." "Cooking it in a lion" is literal; the referent is unclear. This is a technical passage on alchemy and mercury processing. Many plant and mineral names are uncertain. Here "sharp substances" renders rnon po, which in alchemical texts may mean steel. Mica renders lhang tsher. Zhigajé is a vessel or medium that could not be identified and is given phonetically. Posoncha, karan, chawa, chalga, doba, elaji, kuba, punaka, palataka, baricitaka, kaśiśigru and girikarṇa are given as the text spells them, or as phonetic forms. "Honey-drip," "the one that makes the old young" and "the beautiful night tree" are literal renderings of plant names that could not be identified with confidence. The numbered list beginning "One hero" is obscure, and its grouping is uncertain. Most of the plant, animal and mineral names in the substance lists are obscure. Where the Tibetan is a recognizable Sanskrit transliteration or a standard equivalent, the Sanskrit or common English name is given. Many items, such as gharkara, riunuka, barthiba, the thibya bird, puthi, keso and gandakuri, could not be identified with confidence. These are given phonetically, or translated literally where the Tibetan is ordinary words ("elephant hair," "mountain beauty," "ooze," "the dense one," "pus-smell," "thief"). The item counts stated in the text ("these seven," "these nine") do not always match the number of items listed, and the text was translated as it stands. In the homa section, ཧེམ་ཁུང was read as the evident ཧོམ་ཁུང, "homa pit." "Basin" renders the obscure word bor in the line on the hearth's interior; the sense is uncertain. "Sour liquid" renders rang skyur among the wrathful arghya ingredients, whose exact meaning is unclear. In the closing list of tantras, the word rendered "guhyaka" (kept consistent with earlier pieces) most likely stands here for the Guhyasamāja Tantra. "The two secrets" is left as in the Tibetan; it refers to the secret union practiced in the deity yoga. The disciple's role in the dismissal of the fire god is as the Tibetan states it. The prose following the chapter's opening verses is a word-by-word commentarial gloss embedded in the text. The phrase "divisions of condition are fourfold, fourfold" contains an evident repetition in the Tibetan and has been rendered once. In "rough, cool and oily … respectively for wind, bile and phlegm" the order is translated as the Tibetan gives it, even though it does not match the preceding sentence. The meaning of "the garlanded one" (a type of woman) is translated literally, as the Sanskrit term cannot be identified with certainty. "Bird-faced channel" is likewise a literal rendering. Several syllables are read as evident corrections: མྱོས་ཤིག as མྱོས་ཤིང ("grows intoxicated"), སྲ་བདག as སྲ་བ ("hardness"), and སྔ་མཁོ as སྔ་མ ("as before"). ཨོ་ཙ་ཀ is left as "ocaka"; the disease it names is unidentified. ལྷང་ཚེར is taken as mica (abhraka), མུ་ཟི as sulphur, རྣོན་པོ ("the sharp one") as iron or steel (tīkṣṇa), and བ་བླ as orpiment. The four names of mercury are rendered literally after pārada. The posture names (dola, kurbbaṇa, and the rest) are romanized as the Tibetan spells them. Many materia medica and perfume terms here (svasti, badasi/khadasi, the marine "scorpion," chawa, naṅga, rana, chatiba, sehali, the jīva and stamba jewels) could not be identified with confidence and are transliterated or rendered literally; badasi and khadasi are evidently musk-pod varieties. ཞ་ཉེ and རོ་ཉེ are taken as lead and tin respectively. སྨྱུང་པོ, one of the named varieties of myrobalan (the list continues in the next piece), could not be securely matched to a Sanskrit name and is translated literally as "the Fasting One." The mercury myth, mercury as a divine fluid that melted into drops and was scattered, echoes the Indian alchemical account of mercury as divine seed. The seven kinds of harītakī are given their Sanskrit names (pūtanā, rohiṇī, vijayā, cetakī, abhayā, jīvantī, amṛtā) on the strength of the matching descriptions in Indian medical literature: three lines for cetakī, five for abhayā, golden for jīvantī, fleshy for amṛtā, fleshless for pūtanā, gourd-shaped for vijayā.

Several plant names and liquor names in the recipes could not be identified with confidence and are given in simple phonetics or by literal meaning. These are nyaducan, posoncha, sahara datri and datri (possibly dhātakī flowers, a standard fermentation agent), duhika, shashi, katam, shingwangchuk, phurmo, kumpa, jibo, Gyönchen, Zanmo and Nyachichen. The grouping of the eight peṣṭhī and seven godi liquors in the Tibetan does not yield an exact count, and it is rendered as it stands. This is an alchemical and pharmacological section. Many plant, mineral and animal names are given in Tibetan transcription of Sanskrit, some irregular, and have been kept close to the spelling (khadasi, sehalle, kusuma, sihla, kuca, katumaja, madu, posön). "Khadasi" is probably the civet (khaṭṭāśa), whose scent gland is meant. Several Tibetan terms could not be identified with confidence and are given phonetically or literally: "sings po" (rendered sour rice-water), "gca' ba" (cawa), "lhang tsher" (lhangtser, a mineral), and "lto skyes" (belly-born). "Spangs" in the khadasi recipe is read as "sbyangs," "purified." The frog, honey, bee, elephant and lion in the device verses appear to be coded stages in the processing of mercury and are translated literally. In the word-glosses, "dngul" (silver) may stand for quicksilver. This is a section on alchemy and medicine. Many plant and mineral names are transliterations or code words whose identity is uncertain. "Thistle" renders lhang tsher, possibly Echinops. Nāgapar, karikesa, dzak, salila, jala, bathar, pata, viśaca, titraca, shugé, pūti, "white ta", kusuma and sepala are given as they sound.

"The fine substance" (phra mo), "the Grandfather" (mes po, perhaps Pitāmaha as a code name for a plant), "the chief of the Bhūtas", "the sharp-scented one" (dri rnon) and "thin buttermilk" (sings po) are also uncertain.

"Make it swoon" and "killed" render the technical alchemical operations on mercury (mūrcchana and māraṇa). "Great flesh" and "great fat" are the tantric terms for human flesh and fat.

Sang (srang) and zho are Tibetan weight units, a zho being one tenth of a sang. "The number of the Vedas" means four.

The line on Viśveśvara and "the mind" being turned back is obscure as written. Much of this passage uses the coded language of the yoginī tantras (for example "the impurity of the lotus," "the flower of the Beautiful One," "fragrant water," "the great flesh," "the water of the Bhūta"); these have been rendered literally rather than decoded. Several plant and drug names are unidentified and are given in Tibetan or Sanskrit-like form as written: marakada, mahāhataka, ligbumik, murba, mangsitsa, smyungpo, susira. The line rendered "the flesh of a boy and of a young maiden" reads khye'u ngad, which yields no sense; it has been taken as a slip for khye'u dang "a boy and". In the section on the five, the stout one, breastless one, short one, long one, wrathful one, youngest, and ring finger may refer to types of women or to fingers; the text is ambiguous and is translated literally. This is a pharmacological and cosmetic section. Many ingredient names are transliterated Indian plant names that cannot all be identified with confidence. Kantelavana, krimala, elaji, pridar, panamala, aprada, dipuṣpa, kida, matsandi, dasi, palava, kepartī, lajub, rama, sarpodka, atapari, sihlā, sonchika and paṃśṭhi are given as spelled. The terms rendered "the water plant", "indra", "the extractor", "red ralpa", "ever-smoke", "the one that brings harm" and "the sprout of the head" are literal renderings of unidentified plant names. "The Vajra Holder" here very likely refers to the plant vajrī (a spurge), not a deity. In "afterward anoint the teeth with costus", the Tibetan reads tu rta, taken as the evident ru rta, costus. The measure srang is rendered pala. In this chapter's recipes the substances are given in a code language of transliterated syllables and ordinary Tibetan words, such as "king," "minister," "fear," "king's root" and "lord of the earth." Ordinary Tibetan words are translated literally, and syllable codes are romanized, since their referents cannot be identified with confidence. "Vasuta," the nāga lord serving as bowstring, may stand for Vāsuki, but the Tibetan only transliterates the name. The phrase rendered "traces of the mudrā" is literally "large wound-lines by the mudrā," and its sense is uncertain. In "the piercing of the navel," the text reads "breath" (dbugs). This is taken as a slip for the visually similar "piercing" ('bugs), which runs through the surrounding lines. "Vajrarāga" renders rdo rje chags pa, taken as the deity's name; it could also be read as "vajra passion." "Padmanarteśvara" renders padma gar dbang; the phrase could alternatively mean "having become the lotus lord of dance." The deity rendered Padmanarteśvara appears in the Tibetan as "the one who plays with the lotus"; the identification rests on the standard set of six families in Heruka literature (Vajrasattva, Vairocana, Padmanarteśvara, Vajrasūrya, Paramāśva, Heruka). The phrase rendered "vital essence of the body" reads unclearly and is uncertain. In the last sentence the Tibetan has an extra syllable "me" before svara; it has been treated as a slip. "Mātṛkās" in the verses on empowerment refers here to the letters of the alphabet beginning with a. Much of this passage is coded language for sexual yoga and its substances. The line "this is not the knowing of the Victor's very reality" is terse in the Tibetan. It appears to mean that the accomplishments gained through contrived, outer practice fall short of direct knowledge of the Victor's true nature, but the construction is open to other readings. "Lacking completion" may refer to the completion stage.
